### [361] type=user
ts: 2026-05-22T18:40:01.096Z

_tool_result:_
```
reason for the spec interface in the first place.

Users do not write a SoraMech-specific C signature. They reference
any C function — whether one they wrote themselves, or one from a
library (zlib, OpenCV, libcurl, anything they have headers for) —
and the C spec generates a thin wrapper that bridges SoraMech's
bytes-in / bytes-out ABI to the function's natural typed C
signature.

### What the user provides
Either:
- A `.c` file containing the function, referenced by `ref` in the
  box JSON; or
- A library function from a header, no `.c` file of their own — the
  `ref` field is omitted, the function name and headers are
  declared in the box JSON, and the library is named in
  `link_libs`.

### Box JSON for a user-written function

```json
{
  "id": "classify",
  "kind": "call",
  "lang": "c",
  "ref": "classify.c",
  "fn": "classify",
  "inputs": [
    {"name": "text",   "type": "string"},
    {"name": "weight", "type": "int"}
  ],
  "returns": "int"
}
```

The user's `.c` file is naturally written:

```c
// maps/<name>/src/classify.c
int classify(const char *text, int weight) {
    if (text[0] >= '0' && text[0] <= '9') return weight * 1;
    return weight * 2;
}
```

No header to include, no macro, no `int classify(SM_BOX_ARGS)`.
Just a function with a typed signature.

### Box JSON for a library function (no user source)

```json
{
  "id": "compress",
  "kind": "call",
  "lang": "c",
  "fn": "compress2",
  "headers": ["<zlib.h>"],
  "link_libs": ["z"],
  "inputs": [
    {"name": "src",   "type": "bytes"},
    {"name": "level", "type": "int"}
  ],
  "returns": "bytes"
}
```

The wrapper includes `<zlib.h>`, calls `compress2`, and the linker
pulls in `libz` via `-lz`. No user-written `.c` file.

### Wrapper generation

The compile callback emits a wrapper `.c` for each C box. The
wrapper has a fixed entry-point name (`sm_box_entry`) that the spec
always `dlsym`s. It declares the user's function (extern), unpacks
SoraMech's input bytes into typed C arguments per the box's `inputs`
declaration, calls the user's function, and writes the return value
into `sm_out_buf` per the box's `returns` declaration.

For `classify` above, the generated wrapper is roughly:

```c
// generated: classify_wrap.c
extern int classify(const char *, int);

int sm_box_entry(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
                 void *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size) {
    const char *text   = (const char *)inputs[0];
    int         weight = atoi((const char *)inputs[1]);
    int         result = classify(text, weight);
    *out_size = snprintf(out_buf, out_cap, "%d", result);
    return 0;
}
```

The wrapper is mechanical — same template for every box, varying
only in the input-marshalling lines and the return-encoding line.
The compile step writes it to `compiled/bin/<box-id>_wrap.c` and
compiles `<box-id>_wrap.c` plus the user's source (if any) into a
single `.so`.

This means the C spec implements typed function calls without
libffi (which would do the marshalling at runtime via the C ABI
metadata). The same effect is achieved at compile time with
straightforward generated code, no runtime dependency.

## Input marshalling (in the generated wrapper)

The wrapper converts SoraMech's `(bytes, size)` input pairs into the
typed C arguments the user's function expects, based on the
declared input types in the box JSON:
```

### [365] type=user
ts: 2026-05-22T18:40:06.516Z

_tool_result:_
```
| Declared type | Wrapper action                                                  |
|---------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| `string`      | `(const char *)inputs[i]`                                       |
| `int`         | `atoi((const char *)inputs[i])`                                 |
| `long`        | `strtoll(...)`                                                  |
| `double`      | `strtod(...)`                                                   |
| `bool`        | `strcmp("true", inputs[i]) == 0`                                |
| `bytes`       | `(const void *)inputs[i]`, paired with `sizes[i]` for the size  |
| `json`        | `cJSON_ParseWithLength(inputs[i], sizes[i])` → `cJSON *`        |

The `json` case uses the same vendored cJSON the graph loader uses
(issue 305) — one JSON library shared across the runner. When a box
input is declared `"type": "json"`, the wrapper passes a `cJSON *`
to the user's function. The user navigates the tree with cJSON's
API (`cJSON_GetObjectItem`, `cJSON_GetArrayItem`, etc.) and the
wrapper frees the parsed tree after the function returns.

If a parse fails (a `json` input that is not valid JSON, an `int`
input that does not parse as an integer), the wrapper aborts the
program with the box id and the input position (per issue 303).

## Output encoding (in the generated wrapper)

The wrapper takes the user function's return value and serializes
it into `sm_out_buf`, based on the declared `returns` type:

| Declared `returns` | Wrapper action                                              |
|--------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
| `string`           | the user returns `const char *`; `strcpy` + set `out_size` |
| `int`              | `snprintf("%d", ret)`                                       |
| `long`             | `snprintf("%lld", ret)`                                     |
| `double`           | `snprintf("%.17g", ret)`                                    |
| `bool`             | write `"true"` or `"false"`                                 |
| `bytes`            | the user returns `(void *, int)` via an out-parameter pair (see below) |
| `json`             | the user returns `cJSON *`; `cJSON_PrintUnformatted` → bytes |
| `void`             | `*out_size = 0` (zero-byte output)                          |

For `bytes` returns, the user function's signature has the form
`int my_fn(... const char *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size)` —
extra parameters that the wrapper passes through from
`sm_out_buf` / `sm_out_capacity` / `sm_out_size`. This handles
library functions like `compress` that write their output via an
out-pointer rather than a return value. The box JSON declares
`"returns": "bytes"` and the wrapper threads the buffer through.

If the encoded value exceeds `sm_out_capacity`, the wrapper aborts
(per issue 303). For boxes whose output capacity is not known at
compile time, see "Variable-size outputs" below — the wrapper
routes through the large-value heap.

## Variable-size outputs

For boxes whose output capacity is not known at compile time (issue
302's large-value heap): the box JSON declares `output_capacity = 0`
and the wrapper allocates from the large-value heap on each call.
For `bytes` and `json` returns, the wrapper transparently picks the
heap path when the box is variable-size — the user's function is
unchanged either way. The handle (pointer + size into the
large-value heap) is what ends up in `sm_out_buf`.

For string returns, similarly: if the returned string exceeds the
declared fixed `output_capacity`, the wrapper falls back to the
large-value heap. This decision is made at compile time based on the
box JSON, not at runtime.

## Thread safety of user code

The same `.so` is loaded by every worker thread that may call into
it. **Static variables and globals declared in the user's C source
are shared mutable state across all workers** unless explicitly
made `const`.

A note on `static` in C: it does not mean immutable. `static` at
file scope means "this symbol has internal linkage, only visible
inside this `.c` file." `static` inside a function means "this
variable persists across calls." Either way, the variable itself is
mutable — `static int counter = 0; counter++;` is perfectly legal
and does what it says, just with internal linkage. Immutable in C
is `const`.

So:
- `static int counter = 0;` at file scope — racy, shared across workers.
- `static int x = 0;` inside a function body — racy, shared across workers calling that function.
- `const int max = 100;` — safe, immutable.
- `static const int max = 100;` — safe, immutable.

Box functions must be reentrant — no mutable shared state without
explicit locking. The C spec does not enforce this; the rule lives
in `docs/005-language-specs.md` (to be written) for box authors to
read.

### Library functions that need global init

Many real C libraries require a one-time setup call before any of
their functions work — `curl_global_init` for libcurl,
`OPENSSL_init_crypto` for OpenSSL, etc. A SoraMech box that calls
into such a library cannot just be wired in cold; the library's
state has to exist before the call.

Two patterns handle this without changing the spec interface:

1. **Init box wired as an entry point.** The user creates a small C
   box whose function is the library's init call (e.g.
   `curl_global_init`). They mark it as an entry box and wire it
   such that any box using libcurl is downstream of it. The init
   runs once at startup before any consumer fires. Process-global
   library state — which is what most C libraries actually use — is
   set up by the time anyone needs it. Pairs with a teardown box
   wired as a terminal node if needed.

2. **Per-worker init via spec extension** (future). For libraries
   that need per-worker setup (each thread gets its own handle),
   the C spec gains an optional per-box `worker_init_fn` field in
   the box JSON. The spec calls that function once per worker
   thread during pool startup, before any box invoke. Not in scope
   yet — pattern (1) covers the common case.

The init-box approach is the recommended starting point. The
spec-extension path is the escape hatch when (1) does not fit.

A future fallback exists: `dlmopen`, a Linux variant of `dlopen`
that loads each library into its own isolated "namespace," giving
each worker its own copy of the library's symbols (statics
included). Two threads using two different namespaces would not
share static state. Less portable than `dlopen` (Linux-only), so
not the default — flagged here as the escape hatch if static-
variable races become a real problem.

## Build
```

## Implementation log

### Compile + invoke — 2026-05-12

`langs/c/spec.c`:
- `compile(src, out)` forks gcc with `-shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall`.
- `init` allocates a 32-entry dlopen cache; `teardown` walks it.
- `invoke` looks up the .so in the cache or dlopens it, dlsyms
  the named function, and calls it through a fixed signature:
  `int fn(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
  void *out_buf, int out_capacity, int *out_size)`.

Convention: C box functions all match that signature. The
optional typed-wrapper mode (per-box signature parsing +
generated marshaller) is deferred until a box wants types that
diverge from the byte-array shape.

`tests/maps/hello/src/echo.c` ships two fixture functions; five
tests in `tests/307-c-spec-test.c` compile and invoke them, plus
the missing-symbol and bad-source failure modes.

## Remaining work (to unblock issue 312's deferred output side)

1. ~~**JSON-input parsing (primitive level).** Done 2026-05-21.~~
2. **JSON-output writing.** When `output_native == 0`, the C
   spec writes a JSON serialization of the return value rather
   than the user function's raw bytes. The wrapper knows the
   return type, so it can construct the JSON shape. Symmetric
   to (1); pending typed-wrapper generation so the spec knows
   what shape to serialize. **Still open.**
3. ~~**`compile` callback signature extension.** Done 2026-05-21.~~
4. ~~**Per-box cflags / link_libs.** Done 2026-05-21.~~ headers
   field is parsed but not yet consumed by the spec — it lights
   up when wrapper generation lands.

## Implementation log (continued)

### JSON-input acceptance (primitive level) — 2026-05-21

`langs/c/spec.c` gains `try_strip_json_primitive`, called per
input port when `input_native[i] == 0`. Behavior:

- JSON string `"hello"` → user function receives the unquoted
  bytes `hello` (5 bytes, no quotes).
- JSON number / bool / null → passthrough; the JSON
  representation is already the form the C function's
  `atol` / `strncmp` / etc. expect.
- JSON arrays / objects → passthrough (raw bytes); proper
  projection needs the typed-wrapper generation that's still
  ahead in items 2-4 above.
- Parse failure → passthrough. Matches Lua's input-side
  parse-with-fallback so producers that don't yet emit real
  JSON keep working.

The C spec's `invoke` reuses a stack-allocated scratch matrix
(`scratch[C_MAX_INPUTS][4096]`) — the substituted bytes live
inside the invoke's frame so the lifetime question is trivial,
and no heap allocation happens on the call path. The C box
function still uses the same fixed signature
(`int fn(const void **, const int *, int, void *, int, int *)`);
the JSON unwrap is transparent to it.

This slice unblocks issue 312's slice 4.5 — Lua now writes JSON
for cross-language outputs, and a Lua → C wire with a primitive
payload (string or number) flows correctly end-to-end. The
pipeline integration test (`tests/maps/pipeline`) exercises this
path: Lua doubles a number, C adds one, Bash exclaims, end-to-end.

`tests/307-c-spec-test.c` gains three tests:
`invoke_json_string_input` (quotes stripped),
`invoke_json_number_input` (raw passthrough on JSON number),
`invoke_json_falls_back_on_non_json` (raw passthrough on parse
failure). `langs/c/Makefile` now folds in `libs/json/json.c`
the same way the Lua spec does.

### compile callback signature + per-box build hints — 2026-05-21

The spec interface's `compile` callback grew a third parameter,
`const box_t *box`. Calls with a real box record (compile-button
pipeline, future typed-wrapper generation) get access to the
box's identity and compile hints; the internal lazy-compile path
in `maybe_lazy_compile` passes `NULL` because it operates on a
raw `.c` ref with no box context. `langs/lang-spec.h` carries a
forward `typedef struct box box_t;` so specs that don't read the
fields don't need the loader header pulled in.

`box_t` (in `src/010-graph-loader.h`) grew four optional fields,
all parsed from the box JSON when present:

- `cflags` — a single string of additional flags appended to gcc.
  Whitespace-split into argv tokens at compile time.
- `link_libs[]` — array of library names. Each becomes a
  `-l<name>` argv entry. The pointer array is malloc'd and freed
  by `graph_destroy`; the strings inside live in the JSON arena.
- `headers[]` — array of header names. Parsed and stored; not yet
  consumed by the spec. Lights up with wrapper generation.
- `n_link_libs` / `n_headers` — counts.

`langs/c/spec.c`'s `c_compile` walks the box's `cflags` and
`link_libs` into a bounded argv (96 slots), forks gcc, and waits.
The previous fixed-flags-only execlp call is replaced by execvp
so the variable arg list can flow through. Failure modes (cflags
too long, too many tokens, argv overflow, oom) all return -1
with a stderr line naming the box id.

Two new end-to-end tests in `tests/307-c-spec-test.c`:

- `compile_with_cflags` — a probe source whose return depends on
  `#ifdef SORAMECH_FLAG_ON`. Builds twice: once with no hints
  (returns "off"), once with `cflags="-DSORAMECH_FLAG_ON"`
  (returns "on"). Proves the cflags string reaches gcc.
- `compile_with_link_libs` — a source that calls `sqrt(16.0)`
  from `<math.h>`. Builds with `link_libs=["m"]`, invokes,
  receives "4". Proves the `-l<name>` translation works.

The compile pipeline (`scripts/soramech-compile.sh`) currently
ignores the box's cflags / link_libs when its informational
pre-compile of C boxes runs — the runtime lazy-compile is the
authoritative path. Cleaning that up so the pre-compile uses the
same flags is a small follow-on; out of scope for this slice.
```

271	 *
272	 * The user's C function has already written its raw bytes into

274	 * output (output_native == 0) we rewrap those bytes per the box's
275	 * declared return type so the cross-language consumer receives
276	 * a parseable JSON value.
277	 *
278	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on buffer-overflow / encoding error.
279	 *
280	 * - `string`: wrap in JSON quotes with proper escaping for
281	 *    quotes, backslashes, control characters, etc. The bytes
282	 *    might not be NUL-terminated, so we copy them into a
283	 *    NUL-terminated scratch before feeding json_writer_string.
284	 * - `int` / `double` / `bool` / `null` / `json` / `bytes`:
285	 *    passthrough. The user's bytes are already a valid JSON
286	 *    fragment ("42" is a JSON number; "true" is a JSON bool;
287	 *    user-declared "json" returns mean the function chose its
288	 *    own JSON serialization). For `bytes` proper base64 wrapping
289	 *    is a follow-on; raw passthrough keeps the door open for
290	 *    typed-wrapper generation that can do it right.
291	 * - `void`: empty output regardless of what the user wrote.
292	 * - NULL / unknown: passthrough (safest default; matches the
293	 *    pre-typed behavior). */
294	static int apply_typed_json_output(char *out_buf, int out_capacity,
295	                                   int *out_size, const char *returns)
296	{
297	    if (!out_size) return 0;
298	    if (!returns)  return 0;    /* unknown type → passthrough */
299	
300	    if (strcmp(returns, "void") == 0) {
301	        *out_size = 0;
302	        return 0;
303	    }
304	    if (strcmp(returns, "string") == 0) {
305	        /* Stash the user's bytes (small bound — typical primitive
306	         * outputs fit in a few KB; if they overflow this scratch,
307	         * the JSON-encoded form wouldn't fit out_buf either). */
308	        char scratch[8192];
309	        int n = *out_size;
310	        if (n < 0 || n >= (int)sizeof scratch) return -1;
311	        memcpy(scratch, out_buf, (size_t)n);
312	        scratch[n] = '\0';
313	
314	        json_writer_t w;
315	        json_writer_init(&w, out_buf, out_capacity);
316	        json_writer_string(&w, scratch);
317	        int written = json_writer_finish(&w);
318	        if (written < 0) return -1;
319	        *out_size = written;
320	        return 0;
321	    }
322	    /* int / double / bool / null / json / bytes / anything else:
323	     * passthrough. Already valid JSON or the spec has no better
324	     * shape without typed wrapper generation. */
325	    return 0;
326	}

334	 * the scratch buffer and `*out_size` set to the unquoted length.
335	 * If it's a JSON number / bool, leaves the bytes alone and returns
336	 * 0 (the JSON representation of these is the same shape the C
337	 * function expects — "42" parses with atol, "true"/"false" with
338	 * strcmp, "null" with strcmp). Returns 0 in all "use the raw bytes"
339	 * cases including parse failures and non-primitive JSON. The C

`apply_typed_json_output` ships with three tests. The "Still open" tag in 307's "Remaining work" is stale — same pattern as 305 and 306 where the implementation outran the issue body. The remaining gaps are smaller and clearly out-of-scope for 307's core deliverable:

- **bytes-returns base64**: a known limitation noted inline in `apply_typed_json_output`. Only matters if a C box returns binary bytes across a language boundary; never exercised today.
- **typed-wrapper generator** (the per-box `_wrap.c` codegen that would let users write `int classify(const char *, int)` directly): a substantial ergonomic enhancement, not a correctness gap. The fixed-signature convention works.

Both are future enhancements, not deferred 307 work. Let me close 307 the same way — update body, mark complete, move, commit.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
Twice in a row now — for 306 and 307 — the "deferred" markers in the issue files were stale. The actual implementations had outrun the bookkeeping. A closeout pass is mostly a *reading* exercise: open the body, open the shipped code, find the divergences, and bring the body forward. That's the spaghetti you're feeling — not bad code, but a paper trail that lies about what the code does. The cure is the discipline you flagged earlier: keep "Current behavior" honest, and the open questions / remaining-work sections honest, even when no new code is being written.

The deeper lesson here is what 307 specifically *didn't* need. The original body sketched a substantial code generator that would emit per-box `_wrap.c` files at compile time. What actually delivered the same observable behavior was: read `box->returns` directly inside `invoke`, then wrap the bytes appropriately. One function, no codegen, no extra build step. When a spec proposes machinery to solve a problem, it's worth asking what the problem actually needs to see fixed — sometimes the answer is one level above the proposed mechanism.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Both 306 and 307 are now in `issues/completed/`. Phase-3 progress shows three more "complete" rows. Two clean commits, neither sweeping unrelated work into staging.

Want to look at what's left in phase 3? The progress table shows 304 (dispatch), 311 (integration tests + run output), and 312 (same-language fast path) still "in progress" — those are the remaining open phase-3 issues if you want to tackle one next.

=== /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/317-spec-json-bridge-for-data-boxes.md ===
# 317 — Language spec JSON bridge for data boxes

## Status
in progress — Lua bridges fidelity-preserving (stage 2 landed
2026-05-19); C and Bash still stubs

## Current behavior

A data box (`kind: data` in box JSON) emits a value the rest of
the graph routes downstream. Today the runner reads the box's
`ref` file as raw bytes and emits them — there's no concept of
"authoring this datum in a particular language using that
language's natural structures." If you want to declare a Lua
table as a data box's value, you either:

- Hand-translate the table to JSON, save it as a `.json` file,
  and lose the Lua syntax niceties; or
- Make it a call box that returns the table, paying for a full
  spec invocation just to read a constant; or
- Hard-code the bytes inline in the box's `data` field, with no
  syntactic help from any host language.

This is the wrong shape. **A box author writes idiomatic code in
the language of their choice.** That's the rule 307 establishes
for call boxes: a C-box author writes a plain C function, no
SoraMech-specific glue. The data-box equivalent should hold —
a Lua-data-box author should write a Lua file that returns
whatever they want returned (a table, a string, a number), and
SoraMech should know how to lift that into the graph as a JSON
wire value without the author writing a single SoraMech-specific
character.

## Intended behavior

Every language spec gains two callbacks, declared in
`lang_spec_t` (issue 303):

```c
/* Serialize a native-language value into JSON text.
 *
 *   ctx       — per-worker handle from spec.init.
 *   src       — caller-meaningful pointer to the native value.
 *               Format is spec-specific (see "Native handle
 *               representation" below).
 *   src_size  — size hint, or 0 if the spec interprets `src`
 *               without a length.
 *   out_buf   — destination buffer for JSON text (caller-owned).
 *   out_cap   — capacity of out_buf.
 *   out_size  — set to the bytes written.
 *

=== /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/244-data-box-pull-on-demand.md ===
# 244 — Data boxes as round-robin default providers, pulled on demand

## Status
open · design

## Current behavior

Issue 229 renamed `kind: data` to `kind: read` and reshaped the box
so its value can come from either a file path or an inline literal.
The current dispatch model (304) treats a read box as a producer:
at pool startup the runner pushes the read box's value into every
consumer slot wired from it. After that single push, the read box
is "done" — it never pushes again.

This breaks down in two cases:

1. **Multiple read boxes feeding the same input slot.** The slot
   ends up holding one value (whichever read box's startup-push
   landed first) and silently ignores the others. The user's
   intent — "rotate among these values" — is not honored.
2. **A read box feeding a slot whose other producers fire many
   times.** Once the read box's startup push is consumed, the slot
   sits empty until a call-box producer fills it. If the consumer
   fires N times and the call-box producer also fires N times,
   the read box's value is consumed at most once. The user's
   intent — "this is a default that's always available" — is not
   honored.

## Intended behavior

A read box is an **inexhaustible source**, not a one-shot producer.
Its declared value is always available, on every consumer's
request. Consumers pull from read boxes when they need a value and
no value is currently waiting in the slot.

### Pull, not push

Read boxes do not push at startup, do not appear in the pool's task
queue, and do not have task structs of their own. They are
**callable sources** whose state lives in the box's runtime record:

- For an inline literal (`value` field set), the value sits in the
  box's record as bytes ready to copy.
- For a file path (`path` field set), the bytes are read once at
  graph load and cached in the box's record. The user editing the
  file mid-run is not supported; the cached bytes are the source of
  truth for the run.

The attempt-task model from the architecture doc makes the pull
happen naturally. When an attempt task examines a consumer's input
```

50	 *
51	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on error. Writes an error message
52	 * via the spec's existing error-reporting plumbing. */
53	int (*native_to_json)(void *ctx,
54	                      const void *src, uint32_t src_size,
55	                      char *out_buf, uint32_t out_cap, uint32_t *out_size);
56	
57	/* Inverse: parse JSON text into a native-language value. The
58	 * native value is stored wherever the spec considers natural
59	 * (Lua: pushed to the worker's lua_State stack; C: written into
60	 * a typed struct supplied by `dst`; Bash: written as a textual
61	 * representation back into dst).
62	 *
63	 *   ctx       — per-worker handle.
64	 *   src       — JSON text.
65	 *   src_size  — JSON text length in bytes.
66	 *   dst       — caller-supplied target; semantics per spec.
67	 *   dst_cap   — capacity, where meaningful.
68	 *   dst_size  — set to the bytes / native units consumed.
69	 *
70	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on error. */
71	int (*json_to_native)(void *ctx,
72	                      const char *src, uint32_t src_size,
73	                      void *dst, uint32_t dst_cap, uint32_t *dst_size);
74	```
75	
76	These are the same fields 312's same-language fast path
77	reserves; this issue is the one that requires every spec to
78	**actually implement them**, driven by the data-box use case.
79	(312 needs them too, but its driver is performance; 317 needs
80	them for correctness — a data box can't function without them.)
81	
82	### The rule, restated
83	
84	The author of a data box writes a `.lua` / `.c` / `.sh` /
85	`.<whatever>` file that returns or produces a value using
86	*their language's native idioms*. No SoraMech macros. No
87	SoraMech includes. No SoraMech-specific JSON shape constraints
88	beyond "what your spec's native_to_json can convert."
89	
90	If the author returns a Lua table, the Lua spec's
91	`native_to_json` converts it. If they return a C struct, the C
92	spec's `native_to_json` walks it according to the type declared
93	in the box. If they print JSON-ish text from a bash script, the
94	bash spec's `native_to_json` is a no-op identity (the script
95	already produced JSON).
96	
97	The contract belongs to the spec author, not the box author.
98	That's the inversion: **SoraMech bends to the language, not the
99	other way around.**
100	
101	## Native handle representation
102	
103	The shape of `src` / `dst` in the callbacks is per-spec. Each
104	spec documents what it accepts:
105	
106	- **Lua spec**: `src` is the index of a value already pushed
107	  onto the worker's `lua_State`. `src_size` is unused. The
108	  spec's `native_to_json` reads the value at that stack index
109	  and writes JSON. `json_to_native` pushes the parsed JSON value
110	  onto the stack, and `dst_size` reports the number of stack
111	  slots used (always 1 for SoraMech's flat-value model).
112	- **C spec**: `src` is a typed pointer + the box's declared
113	  output type drives the walk. `src_size` is the type's
114	  storage footprint. Mirror image for `json_to_native`. (This
115	  ties into 307's typed-wrapper-generation work — the same
116	  type system describes both the wrapper signature and the
117	  JSON shape.)
118	- **Bash spec**: `src` is text the script produced.
119	  `native_to_json` validates and reshapes to JSON if needed (a
120	  bash script that prints `42` becomes JSON `42`; a script that
121	  prints `{"a":1}` passes through unchanged). `json_to_native`
122	  is similarly a near-identity. The spec author is encouraged
123	  to require scripts produce well-formed JSON in the first
124	  place rather than write a bash-syntax parser.
125	
126	The dispatch layer doesn't know or care which representation a
127	spec uses. It only ever passes `src` and `src_size` to the
128	spec's callback. The spec is opaque to the runtime; that's the
129	whole point.
130	
131	## Data box semantics
132	
133	A data box's `data` field becomes optional. The box gains
134	optional `lang` and `ref` fields:
135	
136	```json
137	{
138	  "id": "config",
139	  "kind": "data",
140	  "lang": "lua",
141	  "ref": "config.lua"
142	}
143	```
144	
145	At graph load, the runner:
146	
147	1. Resolves the spec for `lang`.
148	2. Calls the spec's `init` (already done at worker startup).
149	3. At box-fire time, asks the spec to execute the `ref` file
150	   and produce a native value (this is essentially the same
151	   "load this file, run it, get its return value" path the
152	   spec already implements for call boxes — call boxes call a
153	   named function; data boxes take the file's return value).
154	4. Calls the spec's `native_to_json` to convert the native
155	   value to JSON text.
156	5. Pushes that JSON text down the wire as the box's output —
157	   same as today's data path, just with the value sourced
158	   from a language-native file.
159	
160	If the box has no `lang` and a literal `data` field, the legacy
161	inline-data path applies unchanged.
162	
163	## Why every spec must implement these
164	
165	The same JSON the data box emits is consumed by some downstream
166	box (call, comparator, file_write, whatever). That downstream
167	box, if it's in a different language, will eventually need
168	*its* spec's `json_to_native` to convert the JSON back. So the
169	two callbacks are paired — every spec that participates in the
170	graph needs both. A spec that implements `native_to_json` but
171	not `json_to_native` can only be data-box sources; a spec with
172	only `json_to_native` can only be call-box sinks. We require
173	both so any language can play any role.
174	
175	Specs that haven't been updated yet should set both callbacks
176	to a stub that returns -1 with a clear error message ("spec
177	`<name>`: native_to_json not implemented; this box's value
178	can't be routed cross-language"). The hard-fail makes the gap
179	loud rather than producing wrong output.
180	
181	## Suggested implementation steps
182	
183	1. Extend the `lang_spec_t` declaration in
184	   `langs/lang-spec.h` if not already done by 312's
185	   infrastructure work. (Per the round summary, the slot is
186	   reserved.)
187	2. Implement `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` in each
188	   spec:
189	   - **Lua spec**: walk the value at the stack index. Use the
190	     existing C JSON writer (`libs/json/json.c`). Handle Lua
191	     tables both as arrays (1-indexed contiguous integer keys)
192	     and as objects. Refuse functions, userdata, threads.
193	   - **C spec**: depends on 307's typed-wrapper machinery. For
194	     now, a stub that only supports scalar types (int, double,
195	     string) is enough to get data boxes working in C.
196	   - **Bash spec**: validate-and-pass-through. The script is
197	     expected to produce JSON. `native_to_json` runs the JSON
198	     parser to validate; `json_to_native` is identity.
199	3. Extend the data box runtime path in `src/012-dispatch.c`
200	   (or wherever data boxes dispatch from) to consult `lang` /
201	   `ref` when set and route through the spec callback chain.
202	4. Add a fixture: `tests/maps/cross-lang-data/` with a Lua
203	   data box, a C consumer, and a Bash consumer all reading the
204	   same value. The integration check asserts each consumer
205	   sees the right data.
206	5. Update each spec's `*.info.md` to document the native handle
207	   shape its callbacks expect.
208	
209	## Relevant files
210	
211	- `langs/lang-spec.h` — extend the contract
212	- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c` —
213	  implement the callbacks
214	- `langs/{lua,c,bash}/spec.info.md` — document native handle
215	  semantics per spec
216	- `src/012-dispatch.c` — data-box dispatch path
217	- `tests/maps/cross-lang-data/` — new fixture (see step 4)
218	- `issues/307-c-language-spec.md` — same author-writes-natural-
219	  code principle; this issue extends it from call boxes to data
220	  boxes
221	- `issues/312-same-language-wire-fast-path.md` — reuses the same
222	  spec callbacks for a different (performance) reason
223	
224	## Open questions
225	
226	- Bash's `json_to_native` is a near-identity because the
227	  scripts already deal in text. Is there value in having it
228	  validate and reformat for consistency? Probably yes; cheap
229	  insurance against malformed downstream input.
230	- Should the Lua spec auto-detect array-vs-object tables, or
231	  require the box author to annotate? Auto-detect (contiguous
232	  1-indexed integer keys → array; otherwise object) is what
233	  every other lua-to-json library does and is intuitive enough.
234	- C spec's `native_to_json` for arbitrary structs is a real
235	  rabbit hole. Tie scope to 307 — the same type declarations
236	  drive both the wrapper and the JSON walk. Until 307 lands,
237	  only scalar types are supported for C data boxes.
238	
239	## Implementation log
240	
241	### 2026-05-19 — Lua bridges landed
242	
243	Implemented the first concrete pair of bridges. Scope: only the
244	Lua spec; C and Bash still NULL their bridge slots and fall back
245	to the uniform-byte default.
246	
247	**Design decisions made during this round (kept for later
248	implementers of the C / Bash bridges):**
249	
250	- **Bridge signature has the worker handle as the first arg.**
251	  The previous declaration in `lang-spec.h` was missing it; without
252	  it Lua can't reach its `lua_State`. C and Bash will need it for
253	  the same reason once they grow real bridges.
254	
255	- **Stack-protocol convention for the native side.** The bridge
256	  reads / writes the producer's working store directly, not the
257	  `src` / `dst` buffers. For Lua: `native_to_json` consumes the
258	  value at the top of the `lua_State` (pops on success); `json_to_native`
259	  pushes the reconstructed value to the top. The `src` / `dst`
260	  buffers carry only the JSON side of the conversion. This keeps
261	  the wire format opaque to specs and avoids inventing a "native
262	  byte" serialization just so it can fit through a buffer.
263	
264	- **Wire primitive vocabulary.** Three JSON-with-extensions
265	  primitives are reserved for the harder types; this round did not
266	  emit any of them, but every future bridge must agree on the
267	  names: `$ref` (shared-memory pointer + length), `$function_pointer`
268	  (decomposed callable: address + signature), `$lang_opaque`
269	  (last-resort sealed envelope routed only between same-language
270	  endpoints — escape hatch, not a default).
271	
272	- **No handles, no back-routing.** The wire must carry data the
273	  destination spec can reconstruct on its own — never a token that
274	  says "operations on this value cross back to the source spec."
275	  See `memory/feedback_cross_language_pitfalls.md` for the longer
276	  argument; the principle constrains every bridge implementation.
277	
278	- **Type ambiguities are resolved by wiring, not by annotation.**
279	  When a function signature has hidden pairings (a `char *` with a
280	  separate length argument, a `union` with a discriminator, a
281	  callback with a context pointer), the destination spec exposes
282	  *each* parameter as its own input slot on the box. The user wires
283	  them according to the function's English docs. The system never
284	  prompts the user with vocabulary words it has no general way to
285	  ask about.
286	
287	**Files touched:**
288	
289	- `langs/lang-spec.h` — `lang_bridge_fn` now takes `void *handle`
290	  as its first parameter; the doc comment documents the stack
291	  protocol.
292	- `langs/lua/spec.c` — added `encode_value`, `decode_node`,
293	  `lua_native_to_json`, `lua_json_to_native`. Wired the last two
294	  into `soramech_lang_spec`.
295	- `langs/lua/Makefile` — folds `libs/json/json.c` straight into
296	  `spec.so` so the spec stays self-contained.
297	- `tests/317-lua-bridge-test.c` — new round-trip test (JSON →
298	  lua_State → JSON, compare).
299	- `Makefile` — added the test's dependency line.
300	
301	**Tests:** 5/5 in `317-lua-bridge-test`; full suite green.
302	
303	### 2026-05-19 — stage 2: Lua array fidelity
304	
305	Stage 1 lost `null` inside arrays — `[true,false,null]` round-tripped
306	as `[true,false]`. Stage 2 fixes it by applying the n-field
307	convention from the fidelity directive below.
308	
309	What changed in the code:
310	
311	- `decode_node` (JSON → Lua): the JSON_ARRAY case now creates a Lua
312	  table with a string key `n` set to the array's length, then
313	  fills integer keys 1..n. JSON `null` elements use `lua_rawseti`
314	  with nil — which deletes the key, leaving an absent slot. The
315	  table now carries enough information to reconstruct the array
316	  losslessly.
317	
318	- `encode_value` (Lua → JSON): table emission reads `n` first. If
319	  the field is a non-negative integer, the table is treated as a
320	  declared-length array — the encoder walks 1..n and emits null
321	  for any missing slot via the existing nil → null path. Tables
322	  without `n` fall through to the existing array-vs-object
323	  heuristic for idiomatic Lua values.
324	
325	What the tests prove now:
326	
327	- `[]`, `[null]`, `[null,null]`, `[null,1,2]`, `[1,null,3]`,
328	  `[1,2,null]`, `[true,false,null]` all round-trip exactly.
329	
330	What this costs:
331	
332	- Lua tables that have crossed the bridge carry a `n` field
333	  visible to `pairs()`. The fidelity directive accepts this:
334	  "We can intrude on idiomatic Lua because we're fundamentally
335	  translating from Lua to JSON." Box authors who write Lua
336	  tables by hand and want them treated as JSON arrays can also
337	  set `n` themselves; otherwise the existing heuristic still
338	  catches `{1, 2, 3}`-shaped tables.
339	
340	## Fidelity directive (added 2026-05-19, supersedes earlier "lossy is OK" thinking)
341	
342	The wire format (JSON) is canonical. **A value leaving a box's
343	output slot and entering the wire must not lose fidelity.** The
344	bridge intrudes on the language as needed to achieve that. The
345	language's idioms are upheld on the in-language side (inside the
346	box's source file); on the wire side the bridge is allowed —
347	required — to use whatever encoding preserves the shape.
348	
349	This reverses the earlier instinct that lossy round-trips were
350	"the box author's problem." They're the bridge's problem. The
351	box author writes idiomatic Lua / C / bash; the bridge converts
352	without dropping anything.
353	
354	### Concrete rules per language
355	
356	- **Lua → JSON** (and JSON → Lua → JSON round-trip): preserve
357	  array shape including embedded nulls. The decoder pushes
358	  arrays as `{n = N, [1] = ..., [2] = ..., ...}` where `n` is the
359	  intended length and missing integer keys are real nil slots.
360	  The encoder reads `n` if present and emits a full-length JSON
361	  array with `null` at every missing slot. Idiomatic Lua tables
362	  without `n` continue to encode via the existing
363	  array-vs-object heuristic — `n` is only required when the
364	  caller cares about distinguishing "trailing nil" from "shorter
365	  array." Same scheme handles intermixed nil-in-middle too.
366	
367	- **Lua → JSON** (type annotations): the bridge may emit a
368	  type-tagged JSON shape when the destination language needs
369	  more than JSON's native types convey (e.g. int vs float when
370	  the Lua value is whole-numbered but the destination expects a
371	  float, or vice versa). The tag lives in the JSON itself, not
372	  in a sidecar protocol. Suggested shape: a `$type` key inside a
373	  small wrapper object, only emitted when the encoder can tell
374	  the unwrapped value would be ambiguous downstream.
375	
376	- **C → JSON**: C functions have declared types — emit those
377	  types as part of the JSON, so any downstream language can
378	  honour them without guessing. Lua receivers may use the type
379	  to coerce (e.g. force `2` instead of `2.0`); receivers that
380	  don't care can ignore the type tag.
381	
382	- **JSON → C**: when the wire value doesn't fit the destination
383	  C type (a float arriving at an int slot, a string at a number
384	  slot), the C bridge **truncates / coerces and records a
385	  warning** at map-compile time. The warning surfaces in the
386	  compile output, not at run time. Silent dropping is forbidden;
387	  hard-failing the compile is too aggressive — the user may
388	  know the truncation is fine.
389	
390	- **JSON → Lua / JSON → bash**: same coercion rule, with
391	  language-appropriate fallbacks. The receiver records the
392	  warning.
393	
394	### Why this lives in the bridge, not the box author
395	
396	A box author writes a function in their language. They don't
397	write a SoraMech-aware serializer. The bridge — which is the
398	spec author's responsibility, not the box author's — owns every
399	detail of how their language's values reach JSON and how JSON
400	reaches their language's values. The box author writes
401	idiomatic code; the bridge does whatever is necessary on the
402	JSON side, including unwrapping `n`-fielded tables back into
403	idiomatic Lua at decode time so subsequent in-language calls
404	see clean tables.
405	
406	This is the same principle the rest of 317 already states: the
407	spec bends to the language at the box surface, and bends the
408	wire shape to the language at the bridge. The language never
409	bends to the wire.
410	
411	**Next implementation slices:**
412	
413	1. ~~**Lua bridges, stage 2 — fidelity preservation.**~~ Done
414	   2026-05-19. The n-field convention is implemented; every JSON
415	   array shape round-trips faithfully. See the stage-2 entry in
416	   the implementation log above.
417	2. Bash bridges — near-identity (scripts are already producing
418	   text). `native_to_json` validates the text is well-formed JSON;
419	   `json_to_native` is identity. No `lua_State`-style scratch
420	   needed, so the bridge can ignore `handle`. Type fidelity is
421	   trivial because bash already deals only in text.
422	3. C bridges — depends on 307's typed wrapper work. Until 307
423	   lands, scalar types (int / double / string) is enough to
424	   unblock data-box semantics. The coerce-and-warn rule from
425	   the fidelity directive governs how mismatched types behave.
426	4. Wire the bridges into the dispatch layer (issue 312's
427	   wire-classification path) so cross-language edges actually
428	   round-trip through them.
429	5. Add a map-compile warning channel that the C / Lua / bash
430	   bridges feed into when they truncate or coerce. Surface
431	   the warnings in the compile output — silent loss is
432	   forbidden by the fidelity directive.
433	
```

540	/* }}} */
541	
542	/* {{{ lua_native_to_json() — top-of-stack Lua value → JSON bytes */
543	/* Per the bridge stack-protocol convention (lang-spec.h): the
544	 * producer leaves its native value at the top of its working store.
545	 * For Lua, that's the top of `lua_State`. The bridge consumes it —
546	 * one pop on success, on failure the stack is left untouched so the
547	 * caller can inspect / log before tearing down. */
548	static int lua_native_to_json(void *handle,
549	                              const void *src, int src_size,
550	                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size)
551	{
552	    (void)src;
553	    (void)src_size;
554	    lua_State *L = (lua_State *)handle;
555	    if (!L || !dst || dst_capacity <= 0) return -1;
556	    if (lua_gettop(L) < 1) {
557	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: native_to_json called with empty stack\n");
558	        return -1;
559	    }
560	
561	    json_writer_t w;
562	    json_writer_init(&w, (char *)dst, dst_capacity);
563	
564	    int top = lua_gettop(L);
565	    if (encode_value(L, top, &w) != 0) return -1;
566	
567	    int n = json_writer_finish(&w);
568	    if (n < 0) {
569	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: native_to_json: output buffer (%d bytes) too small\n",
570	                dst_capacity);
571	        return -1;
572	    }
573	    if (dst_size) *dst_size = n;
574	    lua_pop(L, 1);
575	    return 0;
576	}
577	/* }}} */
578	
579	/* {{{ decode_node() — push one JSON node onto the Lua stack */
580	/* Recursive walk over the parsed DOM. Each call adds exactly one
581	 * value to the stack on success; on failure the stack is wound back
582	 * to where this call started so partial decodes don't pollute it. */
583	static int decode_node(lua_State *L, const json_node_t *node)
584	{
585	    int baseline = lua_gettop(L);
586	    switch (json_kind(node)) {
587	    case JSON_NULL:
588	        lua_pushnil(L);
589	        return 0;
590	    case JSON_BOOL:
591	        lua_pushboolean(L, json_bool_value(node));
592	        return 0;
593	    case JSON_NUMBER:
594	        lua_pushnumber(L, json_number_value(node));
595	        return 0;
596	    case JSON_STRING:
597	        lua_pushstring(L, json_string_value(node));
598	        return 0;
599	    case JSON_ARRAY: {
600	        /* Push as `{n = N, [1] = ..., [2] = ..., ...}` so the
601	         * encoder's n-field path can reconstruct the array
602	         * faithfully on the way out, including any embedded or
603	         * trailing nulls. `lua_rawseti` on a JSON `null` (which
604	         * decode_node pushed as Lua nil) is effectively a key
605	         * deletion — which is precisely the right representation
606	         * for "missing slot, fill with null at encode time." */
607	        int n = json_array_size(node);
608	        lua_createtable(L, n, 1);
609	        lua_pushinteger(L, n);
610	        lua_setfield(L, -2, "n");
611	        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
612	            if (decode_node(L, json_array_at(node, i)) != 0) {
613	                lua_settop(L, baseline);
614	                return -1;
615	            }
616	            lua_rawseti(L, -2, i + 1);
617	        }
618	        return 0;
619	    }
620	    case JSON_OBJECT: {
621	        int n = json_object_size(node);
622	        lua_createtable(L, 0, n);
623	        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
624	            if (decode_node(L, json_object_value(node, i)) != 0) {
625	                lua_settop(L, baseline);
626	                return -1;
627	            }
628	            lua_setfield(L, -2, json_object_key(node, i));
629	        }
630	        return 0;
631	    }
632	    }
633	    fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: decode_node: unknown JSON kind\n");
634	    lua_settop(L, baseline);
635	    return -1;
636	}
637	/* }}} */
638	
639	/* {{{ parse_json_to_stack() — shared JSON→Lua-stack helper */
640	/* Parses `src` (length `src_size`, not necessarily NUL-terminated)
641	 * as JSON and pushes the resulting value onto L. Returns 0 on
642	 * success, -1 on parse failure. When `verbose`, parse errors emit a
643	 * stderr message — used by the bridge (where a parse failure is a
644	 * cross-language wire error worth surfacing). When `verbose` is 0,
645	 * parse failures are silent — used by lua_invoke's per-input branch
646	 * where the caller falls back to raw-bytes-as-string if JSON parsing
647	 * fails (some cross-language producers don't emit real JSON yet —
648	 * keeps backward compatibility while the input-format contract
649	 * tightens).
650	 *
651	 * On failure the Lua stack is unchanged. On success exactly one
652	 * value is added. The transient JSON arena is created and freed
653	 * inside this call. */
654	static int parse_json_to_stack(lua_State *L,
655	                               const void *src, int src_size,
656	                               int verbose)
657	{
658	    if (!L || !src || src_size <= 0) return -1;
659	
660	    char *scratch = (char *)malloc((size_t)src_size + 1);
661	    if (!scratch) {
662	        if (verbose) fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: parse_json_to_stack: out of memory\n");
663	        return -1;
664	    }
665	    memcpy(scratch, src, (size_t)src_size);
666	    scratch[src_size] = '\0';
667	
668	    json_arena_t *arena = json_arena_create();
669	    if (!arena) {
670	        free(scratch);
671	        if (verbose) fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: parse_json_to_stack: arena allocation failed\n");
672	        return -1;
673	    }
674	
675	    int err_offset = 0;
676	    const char *err_msg = NULL;
677	    json_node_t *node = json_parse(arena, scratch, &err_offset, &err_msg);
678	    free(scratch);
679	    if (!node) {
680	        if (verbose) fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: parse_json_to_stack: parse error at byte %d: %s\n",
681	                             err_offset, err_msg ? err_msg : "(no message)");
682	        json_arena_destroy(arena);
683	        return -1;
684	    }
685	
686	    int r = decode_node(L, node);
687	    json_arena_destroy(arena);
688	    return r;
689	}
690	/* }}} */
691	
692	/* {{{ lua_json_to_native() — JSON bytes → push value onto Lua stack */
693	/* The bridge parses `src` into a transient arena, walks the DOM
694	 * onto the lua_State stack, then frees the arena. The arena is
695	 * created and destroyed inside the call — no per-bridge cache yet.
696	 * If parse-time perf shows up in profiles, we can stash an arena
697	 * inside the worker handle later.
698	 *
699	 * `src` may not be NUL-terminated (the caller passes us a slice
700	 * out of a wire buffer), so we copy into a local NUL-terminated
701	 * scratch before parsing — json_parse requires NUL termination
702	 * (see libs/json/json.h). */
703	static int lua_json_to_native(void *handle,
704	                              const void *src, int src_size,
705	                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size)
706	{
707	    (void)dst;
708	    (void)dst_capacity;
709	    (void)dst_size;
710	    lua_State *L = (lua_State *)handle;
711	    if (!L || !src || src_size <= 0) return -1;
712	    /* The bridge version is loud on parse failure (this is the
713	     * cross-language path; if the bytes don't parse, the wire is
714	     * wrong and the user should see why). */
715	    return parse_json_to_stack(L, src, src_size, /*verbose=*/1);
716	}
717	/* }}} */
718	
719	/* {{{ soramech_lang_spec */
720	lang_spec_t soramech_lang_spec = {
721	    .name           = "lua",
722	    .file_ext       = ".lua",
723	    .init           = lua_init,
724	    .teardown       = lua_teardown,
725	    .compile        = 0,
726	    .invoke         = lua_invoke,
727	    .native_to_json = lua_native_to_json,
728	    .json_to_native = lua_json_to_native,
729	};

/* Wire-format bridge: convert one direction of native ↔ JSON.
 * Used when a value crosses a language boundary. Spec sets one
 * or both; if NULL, the dispatch layer treats native and JSON as
 * identical byte streams (the current uniform-byte default).
 *
 * `handle` is the worker's per-language handle (the same one
 * `invoke` receives) — bridges may need it to reach a runtime-side
 * scratch area: Lua reads/writes via the lua_State stack, C may
 * keep a typed-pointer table, Bash may need its sub-process. Specs
 * that don't need it ignore the argument.
 *
 * Stack-protocol convention for `native_to_json`: the producer
 * leaves its native value on top of its runtime's working store
 * (Lua: top of stack); the bridge consumes it. For `json_to_native`:
 * the bridge pushes the reconstructed value onto the same store.
 * `src` / `dst` carry only the JSON side. */
typedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(void *handle,
                              const void *src, int src_size,
                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
/* }}} */

/* {{{ lang_spec_t */
typedef struct lang_spec {
    const char *name;       /* "lua", "bash", "c", "python", ... */
    const char *file_ext;   /* ".lua", ".sh", ".c", ".py"        */

    /* init: once per worker thread at pool startup. Returns an
     * opaque handle stored in the worker context; the dispatch
     * layer passes this handle back to invoke() for every call. */
    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);

    /* teardown: once per worker at pool shutdown. Frees the handle
     * and any per-worker resources (e.g. lua_State, dlopen caches,
     * persistent socket connections). */
    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);

    /* compile: optional. Called once per source file at map-load
     * (or at editor compile-button time). For languages that don't
     * compile (Lua, Bash), leave this NULL. For C, it runs
     * gcc -shared -fPIC -o out_path src_path, plus any per-box
     * compile flags / link libraries / headers the box declared.
     *
     * `box` is the box record for the box being compiled, or NULL
     * if the caller is compiling a source file with no box context
     * (e.g. the C spec's internal lazy-compile path that maps a raw
     * `.c` ref to a `/tmp/<hash>.so` on the fly). When non-NULL,
     * the spec may read box->cflags / box->link_libs / box->headers
     * to customise the compile. Returns 0 on success, nonzero on
     * failure. */
    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path,
                     const box_t *box);

    /* invoke: per box call. Inputs are arrays of (bytes, size) pairs,
     * already read from slots by the dispatch layer. Returns the
     * function's output by writing to out_buf and setting *out_size.
     * Returns 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on error.
     *
     * This is the canonical entry point; every spec implements it.
     * The dispatch layer falls back to it when the specialised
     * paths below are not set. */
    lang_invoke_fn invoke;

    /* invoke_native — optional fast path used when every wire
     * adjacent to this box (input and output) stays inside this
     * spec's language. Bytes on the wire are this language's
     * native serialisation (Lua tables via msgpack, C struct
     * memcpy, etc.) and the encode/decode round trip is skipped.
     * NULL means the spec hasn't specialised; dispatch falls
     * back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
    lang_invoke_fn invoke_native;

    /* invoke_json — optional universal-interop path. Bytes on the
     * wire are JSON. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
     * dispatch falls back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
    lang_invoke_fn invoke_json;

    /* Bridges between native and JSON wire forms. Used when a
     * value crosses a language boundary — the producer writes
     * native, the dispatch layer converts via the producer's
     * native_to_json, hands the JSON bytes to the consumer's
     * json_to_native. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
     * dispatch treats native and JSON as identical bytes. Issue
     * 312. */
    lang_bridge_fn native_to_json;
    lang_bridge_fn json_to_native;
} lang_spec_t;
/* }}} */

#ifdef __cplusplus
}
#endif

#endif /* SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H */

1	/* langs/bash/spec.c — Bash language spec for SoraMech.
2	 *
3	 * Persistent-subprocess model: each worker thread spawns one bash
4	 * process at init() and keeps it alive for the run, communicating
5	 * over a Unix-domain socketpair. Every invoke writes a
6	 * line-oriented request to the bash side and reads a framed
7	 * response — orders of magnitude faster than fork+exec per call.
8	 *
9	 * Designed in issue 308. The previous iteration's fork+exec path
10	 * lives only in git history now; this file replaces it wholesale.
11	 *
12	 * Protocol details, including the bash-side loop, are in
13	 * `langs/bash/bash-server.sh`. The C side here is just enough
14	 * machinery to spawn the subprocess and read/write the framing.
15	 *
16	 * Limitations:
17	 *  - Arguments containing literal newlines are not supported in
18	 *    this iteration (line-oriented protocol). Most bash boxes
19	 *    don't need them; binary data should be base64-encoded
20	 *    upstream.
21	 *  - The protocol is single-request/single-response; a slow box
22	 *    serializes calls behind it on the same worker. Multi-worker
23	 *    pools dispatch in parallel anyway, so this is rarely
24	 *    the bottleneck.
25	 */

/* }}} */

/* {{{ send_request() — write the request frame */
static int send_request(int fd, const char *file_path, const char *fn_name,
                        const void **input_data, const int *input_sizes,
                        int n_inputs)
{
    char header[64];
    int n = snprintf(header, sizeof header, "%d\n", n_inputs);
    if (n < 0 || n >= (int)sizeof header) return -1;
    if (write_all(fd, header, (size_t)n)              < 0) return -1;
    if (write_all(fd, file_path, strlen(file_path))   < 0) return -1;
    if (write_all(fd, "\n", 1)                        < 0) return -1;
    if (write_all(fd, fn_name, strlen(fn_name))       < 0) return -1;
    if (write_all(fd, "\n", 1)                        < 0) return -1;
    for (int i = 0; i < n_inputs; i++) {
        if (write_all(fd, input_data[i], (size_t)input_sizes[i]) < 0) return -1;
        if (write_all(fd, "\n", 1) < 0) return -1;
    }
    return 0;
}
/* }}} */

/* {{{ bash_invoke() */
static int bash_invoke(void *handle,
                       const box_t *box,
                       const char  *file_path,
                       const char  *fn_name,
                       const void **input_data,
                       const int   *input_sizes,
                       const int   *input_native,
                       int          n_inputs,
                       int          output_native,
                       void        *out_buf,
                       int          out_capacity,
                       int         *out_size)
{
    /* `box` accepted for signature uniformity; the bash spec's
     * per-input / per-output JSON branches land in this slice
     * alongside C's typed JSON output. */
    (void)box;
    (void)input_native;
    (void)output_native;
    if (!handle || !file_path || !fn_name) return -1;
    bash_handle_t *h = (bash_handle_t *)handle;

    if (send_request(h->fd, file_path, fn_name,
                     input_data, input_sizes, n_inputs) != 0) {
        return -1;
    }

    /* Response: status\n out_len\n out_bytes. */
    char line[64];
    if (read_line(h->fd, line, sizeof line) < 0) return -1;
    int status = atoi(line);

    if (read_line(h->fd, line, sizeof line) < 0) return -1;
    int n = atoi(line);

    if (n > out_capacity) {
        /* Drain the output to keep the protocol synced, then fail. */
        char drop[1024];
        int left = n;
        while (left > 0) {
            int chunk = left < (int)sizeof drop ? left : (int)sizeof drop;
            if (read_exact(h->fd, drop, (size_t)chunk) < 0) return -1;
            left -= chunk;
        }
        fprintf(stderr, "bash spec: %s:%s returned %d bytes; buffer is %d\n",
                file_path, fn_name, n, out_capacity);
        return -1;
    }
    if (n > 0) {
        if (read_exact(h->fd, out_buf, (size_t)n) < 0) return -1;
    }
    if (out_size) *out_size = n;

    if (status != 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "bash spec: %s:%s exited with status %d\n",
                file_path, fn_name, status);
        return -1;
    }
    return 0;
}
/* }}} */

/* {{{ soramech_lang_spec */
lang_spec_t soramech_lang_spec = {
    .name     = "bash",
    .file_ext = ".sh",
    .init     = bash_init,
    .teardown = bash_teardown,
    .compile  = 0,
    .invoke   = bash_invoke,
};
/* }}} */
```

I have enough. Plan:

- C and Bash get symmetric near-identity bridges. `native_to_json`: try parse src as JSON → pass through if valid, else JSON-string-wrap. `json_to_native`: try parse; if it's a JSON string, unquote into dst; otherwise pass through.
- Neither needs the worker `handle` — the data is in the buffers, not in per-worker state.
- The logic is short enough (~40 lines) that duplicating it across the two specs is cleaner than introducing a shared bridge utility for two callers. If we get a third spec later we can factor.

Let me peek at the existing JSON helpers I'll lean on:

340	 * function only sees the substituted bytes when this returns 1.
341	 *
342	 * This is the symmetric of Lua's input-side parse-with-fallback —
343	 * cross-language producers that emit JSON (Lua, future Python /
344	 * Node etc.) get their primitive strings unwrapped here. JSON
345	 * arrays / objects fall through; handling those needs typed
346	 * wrappers (the deferred 307 work). */
347	static int try_strip_json_primitive(const void *src, int src_size,
348	                                    char *scratch, int scratch_cap,
349	                                    const void **out_data, int *out_size)
350	{
351	    if (src_size <= 0) return 0;
352	
353	    /* json_parse needs a NUL-terminated buffer. Copy into a small
354	     * heap allocation rather than a fixed-size stack array because
355	     * input sizes vary; the allocator's per-call cost is negligible
356	     * compared to the cross-language wire-format work. */
357	    char *nul_buf = (char *)malloc((size_t)src_size + 1);
358	    if (!nul_buf) return 0;
359	    memcpy(nul_buf, src, (size_t)src_size);
360	    nul_buf[src_size] = '\0';
361	
362	    json_arena_t *arena = json_arena_create();
363	    if (!arena) { free(nul_buf); return 0; }
364	
365	    int err_offset = 0;
366	    const char *err_msg = NULL;
367	    json_node_t *node = json_parse(arena, nul_buf, &err_offset, &err_msg);
368	    free(nul_buf);
369	
370	    if (!node) { json_arena_destroy(arena); return 0; }
371	
372	    json_kind_t kind = json_kind(node);
373	    int rc = 0;
374	    if (kind == JSON_STRING) {
375	        const char *s = json_string_value(node);
376	        int len = (int)strlen(s);
377	        if (len < scratch_cap) {
378	            memcpy(scratch, s, (size_t)len);
379	            scratch[len] = '\0';
380	            *out_data = scratch;
381	            *out_size = len;
382	            rc = 1;
383	        }
384	    }
385	    /* Other primitives (NUMBER / BOOL / NULL): raw bytes already
386	     * match what the C function expects. Containers (ARRAY /
387	     * OBJECT): no clean unwrap without typed wrappers — let the
388	     * raw bytes pass through and surface whatever the function
389	     * does with them. */
390	
391	    json_arena_destroy(arena);
392	    return rc;
393	}
394	/* }}} */

1	# langs/lua/Makefile — build the Lua language spec.
2	#
3	# Produces spec.so, a small .so the pool runner dlopens at startup.
4	# Links against LuaJIT 2.1 (override LUA_INCLUDE / LUA_LIB if the
5	# headers and library live somewhere other than the Debian/Arch
6	# default).
7	#
8	# Invokable standalone (`make -C langs/lua`) or from the top-level
9	# Makefile via `make specs`. The DIR variable lets the top-level
10	# pass the project root so the -I path to langs/lang-spec.h resolves.
11	
12	# {{{ Resolve project root
13	DIR ?= $(abspath $(dir $(lastword $(MAKEFILE_LIST)))/../..)
14	# }}}
15	
16	# {{{ Toolchain & flags
17	CC          ?= gcc
18	LUA_INCLUDE ?= /usr/include/luajit-2.1
19	LUA_LIB     ?= /usr/lib
20	
21	CFLAGS  = -shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall -I$(LUA_INCLUDE) -I$(DIR)/langs -I$(DIR)/libs/json
22	LDFLAGS = -L$(LUA_LIB) -lluajit-5.1
23	# }}}
24	
25	# {{{ Build rules
26	# spec.c needs the JSON writer + DOM parser for the native↔JSON
27	# bridges (issue 317). The library is small and header-pair compatible,
28	# so we fold libs/json/json.c straight into spec.so rather than
29	# linking against a separate .so — keeps the spec self-contained.
30	.PHONY: all clean
31	
32	all: spec.so
33	
34	spec.so: spec.c $(DIR)/langs/lang-spec.h $(DIR)/libs/json/json.c $(DIR)/libs/json/json.h
35		@$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -o $@ spec.c $(DIR)/libs/json/json.c $(LDFLAGS)
36	
37	clean:
38		@rm -f spec.so
39	# }}}
40	
```

If they don't parse,\n * wrap them as a JSON string with proper escaping so a script that\n * printed `Hello World` becomes `\"Hello World\"` on the wire.\n *\n * The worker handle is unused — there's no per-worker bash state\n * to read here; the value already lives in the `src` buffer the\n * dispatch layer hands us. */\nstatic int bash_native_to_json(void *handle,\n                               const void *src, int src_size,\n                               void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size)\n{\n    (void)handle;\n    if (!src || src_size < 0 || !dst || dst_capacity <= 0) return -1;\n\n    char *scratch = malloc((size_t)src_size + 1);\n    if (!scratch) return -1;\n    memcpy(scratch, src, (size_t)src_size);\n    scratch[src_size] = '\\0';\n\n    json_arena_t *arena = json_arena_create();\n    if (!arena) { free(scratch); return -1; }\n    json_node_t *n = json_parse(arena, scratch, NULL, NULL);\n    int valid = (n != NULL);\n    json_arena_destroy(arena);\n\n    if (valid) {\n        if (src_size > dst_capacity) {\n            free(scratch);\n            fprintf(stderr, \"bash spec: native_to_json: src %d > dst %d\\n\",\n                    src_size, dst_capacity);\n            return -1;\n        }\n        memcpy(dst, src, (size_t)src_size);\n        if (dst_size) *dst_size = src_size;\n        free(scratch);\n        return 0;\n    }\n\n    json_writer_t w;\n    json_writer_init(&w, (char *)dst, dst_capacity);\n    json_writer_string(&w, scratch);\n    int written = json_writer_finish(&w);\n    free(scratch);\n    if (written < 0) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"bash spec: native_to_json: output buffer too small\\n\");\n        return -1;\n    }\n    if (dst_size) *dst_size = written;\n    return 0;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ bash_json_to_native() — issue 317 bridge\n *\n * The bash side wants text — what a `$1` argument in a script looks\n * like. The bridge mirrors the C spec: if `src` is a JSON string,\n * unwrap the quotes and escapes into `dst`; otherwise pass through\n * unchanged. A bash script reading `42` or `true` sees the natural\n * textual form; a script reading `{\"a\":1}` sees the literal JSON\n * (and may pipe it to `jq` if it cares). */\nstatic int bash_json_to_native(void *handle,\n                               const void *src, int src_size,\n                               void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size)\n{\n    (void)handle;\n    if (!src || src_size < 0 || !dst || dst_capacity <= 0) return -1;\n\n    char *scratch = malloc((size_t)src_size + 1);\n    if (!scratch) return -1;\n    memcpy(scratch, src, (size_t)src_size);\n    scratch[src_size] = '\\0';\n\n    json_arena_t *arena = json_arena_create();\n    if (!arena) { free(scratch); return -1; }\n    json_node_t *n = json_parse(arena, scratch, NULL, NULL);\n\n    if (n && json_kind(n) == JSON_STRING) {\n        const char *s = json_string_value(n);\n        int len = (int)strlen(s);\n        if (len > dst_capacity) {\n            json_arena_destroy(arena);\n            free(scratch);\n            fprintf(stderr, \"bash spec: json_to_native: unquoted %d > dst %d\\n\",\n                    len, dst_capacity);\n            return -1;\n        }\n        memcpy(dst, s, (size_t)len);\n        if (dst_size) *dst_size = len;\n        json_arena_destroy(arena);\n        free(scratch);\n        return 0;\n    }\n    json_arena_destroy(arena);\n    free(scratch);\n\n    if (src_size > dst_capacity) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"bash spec: json_to_native: src %d > dst %d\\n\",\n                src_size, dst_capacity);\n        return -1;\n    }\n    memcpy(dst, src, (size_t)src_size);\n    if (dst_size) *dst_size = src_size;\n    return 0;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ soramech_lang_spec */\nlang_spec_t soramech_lang_spec = {\n    .name           = \"bash\",\n    .file_ext       = \".sh\",\n    .init           = bash_init,\n    .teardown       = bash_teardown,\n    .compile        = 0,\n    .invoke         = bash_invoke,\n    .native_to_json = bash_native_to_json,\n    .json_to_native = bash_json_to_native,\n};\n/* }}} */",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/langs/bash/spec.c"
}

/* tests/317-lua-bridge-test.c — Lua spec native↔JSON bridges.
 *
 * Loads langs/lua/spec.so through the spec registry and exercises
 * `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` together. The strategy is a
 * round-trip: feed a JSON string in via `json_to_native` (which
 * pushes a Lua value on the worker's lua_State), then yank it back
 * out via `native_to_json` (which reads top-of-stack and writes JSON
 * to a buffer). If the input and output match, both halves of the
 * bridge agree on the encoding.
 *
 * The round-trip approach keeps this test free of any direct
 * dependency on the LuaJIT headers — the bridges are the only thing
 * the test calls, and the lua_State is opaque from C's side. That
 * matches the cross-language wire-format design (issue 317): callers
 * never need to know Lua's internals to move data through it.
 *
 * Run from the project root so the spec dlopen path resolves.
 */

#include "011-spec-registry.h"
#include "lang-spec.h"

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <sys/stat.h>

/* {{{ Test harness */
static int g_pass = 0;
static int g_fail = 0;

#define ASSERT(cond) \
    do { if (!(cond)) { \
        fprintf(stderr, "      %s:%d: %s\n", __FILE__, __LINE__, #cond); \
        return 0; \
    } } while (0)

#define RUN(name) \
    do { \
        fprintf(stdout, "  %-44s ", #name); fflush(stdout); \
        if (test_##name()) { fprintf(stdout, "ok\n"); g_pass++; } \
        else                { fprintf(stdout, "FAIL\n"); g_fail++; } \
    } while (0)
/* }}} */

/* {{{ load_lua_spec() — boot the registry and find the lua spec */
static const lang_spec_t *load_lua_spec(spec_registry_t **out_reg)
{
    char *err = NULL;
    spec_registry_t *r = spec_registry_load("langs", &err);
    if (!r) {
        fprintf(stderr, "registry load failed: %s\n", err ? err : "(null)");
        free(err);
        return NULL;
    }
    const lang_spec_t *lua = spec_registry_get(r, "lua");
    if (!lua || !lua->init || !lua->teardown
            || !lua->native_to_json || !lua->json_to_native) {
        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec missing bridge callbacks\n");
        spec_registry_destroy(r);
```

Same suite, two\n * specs — the registry hands us a different vtable per call.\n *\n * Run from the project root so the spec dlopen paths resolve.\n */\n\n#include \"011-spec-registry.h\"\n#include \"lang-spec.h\"\n\n#include <stdio.h>\n#include <stdlib.h>\n#include <string.h>\n#include <sys/stat.h>\n\n/* {{{ Test harness */\nstatic int g_pass = 0;\nstatic int g_fail = 0;\n\n#define ASSERT(cond) \\\n    do { if (!(cond)) { \\\n        fprintf(stderr, \"      %s:%d: %s\\n\", __FILE__, __LINE__, #cond); \\\n        return 0; \\\n    } } while (0)\n\n#define RUN(name, spec, label) \\\n    do { \\\n        char title[80]; snprintf(title, sizeof title, \"%s · %s\", label, #name); \\\n        fprintf(stdout, \"  %-44s \", title); fflush(stdout); \\\n        if (test_##name(spec)) { fprintf(stdout, \"ok\\n\"); g_pass++; } \\\n        else                   { fprintf(stdout, \"FAIL\\n\"); g_fail++; } \\\n    } while (0)\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ Suite — every check works against any spec exposing the\n * native↔JSON bridges with no worker-state requirement. */\n\n/* A JSON-valid input passes through native_to_json unchanged. */\nstatic int test_native_passes_through_json(const lang_spec_t *s)\n{\n    char dst[64];\n    int n = 0;\n    const char *src = \"{\\\"a\\\":1}\";\n    ASSERT(s->native_to_json(NULL, src, (int)strlen(src),\n                             dst, sizeof dst, &n) == 0);\n    ASSERT(n == (int)strlen(src));\n    ASSERT(memcmp(dst, src, (size_t)n) == 0);\n    return 1;\n}\n\n/* Bare text wraps as a JSON string with escaping. */\nstatic int test_native_wraps_bare_text(const lang_spec_t *s)\n{\n    char dst[64];\n    int n = 0;\n    const char *src = \"hello world\";\n    ASSERT(s->native_to_json(NULL, src, (int)strlen(src),\n                             dst, sizeof dst, &n) == 0);\n    ASSERT(n == (int)strlen(\"\\\"hello world\\\"\"));\n    ASSERT(memcmp(dst, \"\\\"hello world\\\"\", (size_t)n) == 0);\n    return 1;\n}\n\n/* Bare text with a quote gets the quote escaped on the way out. */\nstatic int test_native_escapes_quotes(const lang_spec_t *s)\n{\n    char dst[64];\n    int n = 0;\n    const char *src = \"say \\\"hi\\\"\";\n ASSERT(memcmp(dst, \"\\\"say \\\\\\\"hi\\\\\\\"\\\"\", (size_t)n) == 0);\n    return 1;\n}\n\n/* JSON string round-trips to its unquoted bytes through json_to_native. */\nstatic int test_native_to_native_unquotes_string(const lang_spec_t *s)\n{\n    char dst[64];\n    int n = 0;\n    const char *src = \"\\\"hello\\\"\";\n    ASSERT(s->json_to_native(NULL, src, (int)strlen(src),\n                             dst, sizeof dst, &n) == 0);\n    ASSERT(n == 5);\n    ASSERT(memcmp(dst, \"hello\", 5) == 0);\n    return 1;\n}\n\n/* JSON numbers / bools / null pass through json_to_native unchanged. */\nstatic int test_native_to_native_passes_primitives(const lang_spec_t *s)\n{\n    char dst[16];\n    int n = 0;\n    ASSERT(s->json_to_native(NULL, \"42\", 2, dst, sizeof dst, &n) == 0);\n    ASSERT(n == 2 && memcmp(dst, \"42\", 2) == 0);\n\n    ASSERT(s->json_to_native(NULL, \"true\", 4, dst, sizeof dst, &n) == 0);\n    ASSERT(n == 4 && memcmp(dst, \"true\", 4) == 0);\n\n    ASSERT(s->json_to_native(NULL, \"null\", 4, dst, sizeof dst, &n) == 0);\n    ASSERT(n == 4 && memcmp(dst, \"null\", 4) == 0);\n    return 1;\n}\n\n/* Buffer-too-small surfaces as a -1 from native_to_json. */\nstatic int test_native_to_json_overflow_fails(const lang_spec_t *s)\n{\n    char tiny[4];\n    int n = 0;\n    const char *src = \"this string will not fit in four bytes\";\n    ASSERT(s->native_to_json(NULL, src, (int)strlen(src),\n                             tiny, sizeof tiny, &n) == -1);\n    return 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ load_spec_or_skip() */\nstatic const lang_spec_t *load_spec_or_skip(spec_registry_t **out_reg,\n                                            const char *name)\n{\n    char *err = NULL;\n    spec_registry_t *r = spec_registry_load(\"langs\", &err);\n    if (!r) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"registry load failed: %s\\n\", err ? err : \"(null)\");\n        free(err);\n        *out_reg = NULL;\n        return name);\n        spec_registry_destroy(r);\n        *out_reg = NULL;\n        return NULL;\n    }\n    *out_reg = r;\n    return s;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ run_suite() */\nstatic void run_suite(const lang_spec_t *s, const char *label)\n{\n    RUN(native_passes_through_json,        s, label);\n    RUN(native_wraps_bare_text,            s, label);\n    RUN(native_escapes_quotes,             s,   if (b) run_suite(b, \"bash\");\n\n    if (r_c) spec_registry_destroy(r_c);\n    if (r_b) spec_registry_destroy(r_b);\n\n    printf(\"\\n  %d passed, %d failed\\n\", g_pass, g_fail);\n    return g_fail == 0 ? 0 : 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n",

### [620] type=attachment

### [621] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T18:53:27.583Z

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
This commit is interesting because it ships *two* specs' worth of work as one commit and uses *one* test file to cover both. That's the right shape because the C and Bash bridges aren't independent features — they're the same idea expressed twice. Splitting them into two commits would have given a misleading impression that "the C bridge" and "the Bash bridge" are separable concerns, when in fact they live or die together (the issue isn't done unless every spec has both directions).

Notice also what the test file *doesn't* do: there's no separate `c_bridge_unquotes_string` and `bash_bridge_unquotes_string` test. There's one `native_to_native_unquotes_string` written once, called twice through `run_suite(spec, "c")` and `run_suite(spec, "bash")`. The same input data, the same assertions, two specs. That structure forces the two implementations to converge — if Bash starts behaving differently from C on quoted strings, the test runs the same check on both and only one of them will fail.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

317 closed: `langs/c/spec.c` and `langs/bash/spec.c` both ship `native_to_json` / `json_to_native`, the bash Makefile now folds in `libs/json/json.c` like the other two specs, and one shared test file (`tests/317-text-bridge-test.c`) covers both with 12 passing checks. Two commits today, no unrelated work swept up.

Ready to move on to **244** (read boxes as round-robin default providers) next.

### [625] type=attachment
