### [626] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T18:57:02.157Z

### [627] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T18:57:19.672Z

You're right to push back. My framing was sloppy and points at exactly the wrong architecture.

The C bridge and the Bash bridge **are** independent. Each lives in its own `.so`, dlopened by the registry without knowing the other exists. Cross-language flow runs through JSON as the neutral pivot, so a Lua → C wire works because the Lua spec emits JSON via *its* `native_to_json` and the C spec consumes JSON via *its* `json_to_native` — neither spec ever calls into the other. You could ship the C bridge today and the Bash bridge next month and nothing in the runtime would notice; you could add a Python spec without touching either. That's the whole point of the spec interface, and 317 doesn't change it.

What I should have said was something like: *"the issue 317 deliverable says every spec must implement both directions, so by the issue's own contract it isn't closeable until both specs ship — but each spec is independently deployable and the commit could just as legitimately have been two."* The coupling I described is at the **issue / paperwork** level, not the architectural level. Conflating those is the same category mistake the project has been suffering from — body claims a coupling, code stays clean, future readers get confused about which is load-bearing.

Honestly the cleaner shape would have been:
1. one commit "C spec gains 317 bridges"
2. one commit "Bash spec gains 317 bridges"
3. one commit "shared text-bridge test harness + close 317"

Each commit revertible on its own, each describing a real unit of work. The way I shipped it makes a *future* refactor (extract shared bridge to `libs/json/`, add a Python spec) look like it would touch a coupled blob, when in reality those changes are clean per-spec edits.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
You just caught a thing that happens a lot in commit messages: the writer uses scope-of-issue language ("these belong together") that gets re-read later as architecture language ("these are coupled"). The difference is invisible in the moment but very expensive when someone six months later reads the commit message and concludes the system is more entangled than it is. The fix is a discipline: in commit messages, describe what the runtime does, not what the issue file says. "C spec gains a JSON bridge" is honest. "C and Bash bridges live or die together" is editorializing on top of the issue's bookkeeping, and it lies about the code.

If you want, I can write better next time and/or be explicit about scope-vs-architecture distinctions when summarising. The 317 commit message is already in the history — amending it would just churn the log for a phrasing nit — but the principle should hold for 244 and 312.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Want me to proceed to 244 with that framing kept honest?

_tool_result:_
```
1	# 244 — Data boxes as round-robin default providers, pulled on demand
2	
3	## Status
4	open · design
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	Issue 229 renamed `kind: data` to `kind: read` and reshaped the box
9	so its value can come from either a file path or an inline literal.
10	The current dispatch model (304) treats a read box as a producer:
11	at pool startup the runner pushes the read box's value into every
12	consumer slot wired from it. After that single push, the read box
13	is "done" — it never pushes again.
14	
15	This breaks down in two cases:
16	
17	1. **Multiple read boxes feeding the same input slot.** The slot
18	   ends up holding one value (whichever read box's startup-push
19	   landed first) and silently ignores the others. The user's
20	   intent — "rotate among these values" — is not honored.
21	2. **A read box feeding a slot whose other producers fire many
22	   times.** Once the read box's startup push is consumed, the slot
23	   sits empty until a call-box producer fills it. If the consumer
24	   fires N times and the call-box producer also fires N times,
25	   the read box's value is consumed at most once. The user's
26	   intent — "this is a default that's always available" — is not
27	   honored.
28	
29	## Intended behavior
30	
31	A read box is an **inexhaustible source**, not a one-shot producer.
32	Its declared value is always available, on every consumer's
33	request. Consumers pull from read boxes when they need a value and
34	no value is currently waiting in the slot.
35	
36	### Pull, not push
37	
38	Read boxes do not push at startup, do not appear in the pool's task
39	queue, and do not have task structs of their own. They are
40	**callable sources** whose state lives in the box's runtime record:
41	
42	- For an inline literal (`value` field set), the value sits in the
43	  box's record as bytes ready to copy.
44	- For a file path (`path` field set), the bytes are read once at
45	  graph load and cached in the box's record. The user editing the
46	  file mid-run is not supported; the cached bytes are the source of
47	  truth for the run.
48	
49	The attempt-task model from the architecture doc makes the pull
50	happen naturally. When an attempt task examines a consumer's input
51	slots, the check is now:
52	
53	```
54	for each required input port:
55	   if the slot has a value waiting (ring buffer non-empty):
56	      take that value
57	   elif the port has any read-box predecessor:
58	      round-robin pull from the next read-box predecessor
59	   else:
60	      not ready — return from the attempt
61	```
62	
63	The pull copies the read box's value (or its `$ref` handle, if
64	the value is variable-size) into the consumer's local snapshot.
65	The read box's record is unchanged.
66	
67	### Round-robin across multiple read-box predecessors
68	
69	A consumer input port with N read-box predecessors carries a small
70	piece of state on the consumer box:
71	
72	```
73	input_port_state {
74	   data_box_ids: [box_id, box_id, ...],   // declared order
75	   rr_index:     0                        // next to pull from
76	}
77	```
78	
79	The attempt task uses `data_box_ids[rr_index]`, increments
80	`rr_index` mod `len(data_box_ids)`, and proceeds. Multiple attempt
81	tasks running in parallel for the same consumer use the same
82	counter via the atomic-increment slot operation from 302 — distinct
83	attempts get distinct indices, no race.
84	
85	### Mixed predecessors: read boxes as defaults
86	
87	A consumer port may have a mix of call-box predecessors AND
88	read-box predecessors. The attempt rule (slot-first, read-box-
89	fallback) handles this naturally:
90	
91	- If a call-box producer has pushed a value, the slot is non-empty
92	  and the attempt consumes from there.
93	- If the slot is empty, the attempt pulls from the next read-box
94	  predecessor.
95	
96	So a read box behaves as a **default value** for the slot — used
97	only when no call-box producer has supplied an actual value. This
98	matches the typical use: "supply a fallback host address, but if a
99	call box computes a custom one, use the custom one instead."
100	
101	## Schema implications
102	
103	The compile-time check from 230 currently requires every
104	non-optional input port to be wired, carry a literal, or be marked
105	optional. Under this issue, **a read-box predecessor on the port
106	also satisfies the requirement** — the port is guaranteed to be
107	fed at attempt time, just via pull rather than push.
108	
109	`src/001-schema.lua::check_input_bindings` extends:
110	
111	```
112	state if any of:
113	   has_wire (any producer-class predecessor)        ← unchanged
114	   has_literal (port.value set)                     ← unchanged
115	   has_data_box_predecessor (read-box predecessor)  ← new
116	   is_optional (port.optional == true)              ← unchanged
117	```
118	
119	The editor's compile-or-save validation surfaces the same check;
120	the user sees an error if a required port has none of the above.
121	
122	## Suggested implementation steps
123	
124	1. `src/001-schema.lua::check_input_bindings` — extend to
125	   recognize read-box-as-predecessor as a satisfying state.
126	2. `src/010-graph-loader.c` — at graph load, walk every consumer
127	   box; for each input port, compute the list of read-box
128	   predecessors and store as part of the port's runtime state.
129	   Cache each read box's value bytes (or LVH handle) in the box's
130	   runtime record.
131	3. `src/012-dispatch.c` — the attempt-task's input check
132	   incorporates the read-box pull: slot first, else round-robin
133	   from `data_box_ids[rr_index]`.
134	4. The startup phase no longer spawns tasks for read boxes (or
135	   pushes their values eagerly). Read boxes are graph-load data
136	   only; they do not appear in the pool's queue.
137	5. Fixture: `tests/maps/data-box-defaults/` — a consumer with one
138	   call-box and one read-box predecessor, exercising both "value
139	   available" and "fallback pulled" paths.
140	6. Fixture: `tests/maps/data-box-rotation/` — a consumer with three
141	   read-box predecessors, asserting round-robin order over a series
142	   of attempt invocations.
143	
144	## Cycle behavior
145	
146	Pull-on-demand removes a startup-ordering concern: a graph with a
147	read box feeding two consumers no longer depends on "did the read
148	box's push land before either consumer fired its first attempt?" —
149	the attempts pull when they need to, regardless of order. Iterators
150	downstream of read boxes work cleanly: each iteration's attempt
151	pulls fresh from the read box (or its rotation).
152	
153	## Relevant files
154	
155	- `issues/completed/229-data-box-as-language-agnostic-file-io.md` —
156	  parent; renamed data→read/write. This issue extends the read-box
157	  semantics from "one-shot producer" to "inexhaustible pull-source."
158	- `issues/completed/230-required-inputs-and-optional-flag.md` —
159	  parent; this issue adds a third satisfying state to its check.
160	- `src/001-schema.lua` — extended binding check.
161	- `src/010-graph-loader.c` — per-port read-box-predecessor list
162	  computed at load.
163	- `src/012-dispatch.c` — attempt-task input pull.
164	- `docs/001-architecture.md` — the attempt loop (described in the
165	  "structural shell" section) is the place where the pull happens.
166	
167	## Open questions
168	
169	- **Read-box value mutation during a run.** A file-backed read box
170	  whose file changes mid-run: do we re-read on every pull, or stay
171	  with the load-time cached value? Lean toward "load-time cache"
172	  (deterministic, no I/O per pull, mirrors the immutability of the
173	  graph during a run). Re-read on pull is a follow-on if the use
174	  case appears.
175	- **Round-robin determinism across parallel attempts.** The atomic
176	  counter for `rr_index` gives every attempt a distinct index, but
177	  the order in which attempts complete is non-deterministic. If
178	  attempts A1 and A2 both pull index 0 and index 1 respectively
179	  but A2 finishes first, the consumer's overall output sequence
180	  reflects A2 before A1. This is the same parallel-iterator
181	  ordering question 302's tagged slots already solve. Apply the
182	  same tagging: attempts that pull from read boxes carry the
183	  pulled index as a tag, downstream consumers reorder by tag.
184	
```

162	/* For multi-spawn boxes, we want to re-spawn so long as every POP

165	static int box_pop_ready(const dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, int box_id)
166	{
167	    const box_t *b = graph_box(ctx->graph, box_id);

173	        saw_pop = 1;

175	    }
176	    return saw_pop;
177	}

180	/* {{{ dispatch_spawn() */
181	void dispatch_spawn(const dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, int box_id, int priority)
182	{
183	    if (!ctx || !ctx->pool) return;
184	    dispatch_task_t *t = malloc(sizeof *t);
185	    if (!t) return;
186	
187	    /* Assign task_id at submission so the same id correlates the
188	     * task_submit / task_start / task_end events. The next_task_id
189	     * counter is atomic so concurrent spawners don't collide. */
190	    dispatch_ctx_t *mctx = (dispatch_ctx_t *)ctx;
191	    t->task_id = atomic_fetch_add_explicit(&mctx->next_task_id, 1,
192	                                           memory_order_relaxed);
193	    t->box_id  = box_id;
194	    t->ctx     = ctx;
195	
196	    if (ctx->events) {
197	        const box_t *b = graph_box(ctx->graph, box_id);
198	        event_queue_task_submit(ctx->events, now_secs(),
199	            t->task_id, b ? b->id : "(unknown)", -1);
200	    }
201	    pool_spawn(ctx->pool, dispatch_action, t, priority);
202	}
203	/* }}} */

205	/* {{{ dispatch_spawn_if_ready() */
206	void dispatch_spawn_if_ready(dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, int box_id, int priority)
207	{
208	    if (!ctx) return;
209	    if (box_id < 0 || box_id >= ctx->n_boxes) return;
210	    if (!box_is_ready(ctx, box_id)) return;
211	
212	    const box_t *b = graph_box(ctx->graph, box_id);
213	    if (b && b->multi_spawn) {
214	        /* Multi-spawn box (iterator or anything downstream of one).
215	         * The single-spawn guard doesn't apply: every push that
216	         * leaves the box ready spawns a new task, which will drain
217	         * one cell from each POP input. The iterator's own
218	         * dispatch_action also re-spawns itself while inputs remain
219	         * so a single push can wake the chain. */
220	        dispatch_spawn(ctx, box_id, priority);
221	        return;
222	    }
223	
224	    /* Single-spawn case: CAS the per-box guard. The first push that
225	     * finds the consumer ready wins; everyone else short-circuits.
226	     * The 1-cell peek model spawns each box at most once per run. */
227	    int expected = 0;
228	    if (atomic_compare_exchange_strong(&ctx->box_ever_spawned[box_id],

232	}

The graph holds onto these bytes for the life of\n         * the run. */\n        const char *base = g->map_dir;\n        size_t blen = base ? strlen(base) : 0;\n        size_t plen = strlen(b->path);\n        char *full = malloc(blen + 1 + plen + 1);\n        if (!full) { *err = err_fmt(\"oom\"); return -1; }\n        if (b->path[0] == '/') {\n            memcpy(full, b->path, plen + 1);\n        } else if (blen > 0) {\n            memcpy(full, base, blen);\n            full[blen] = '/';\n            memcpy(full + blen + 1, b->path, plen + 1);\n        } else {\n            memcpy(full, b->path, plen + 1);\n        }\n\n        FILE *fp = fopen(full, \"rb\");\n        if (!fp) {\n            *err = err_fmt(\"read box '%s': cannot open '%s'\", b->id, full);\n            free(full);\n            return -1;\n        }\n        free(full);\n        fseek(fp, 0, SEEK_END);\n        long len = ftell(fp);\n        fseek(fp, 0, SEEK_SET);\n        if (len < 0) {\n            *err = err_fmt(\"read box '%s': ftell failed\", b->id);\n            fclose(fp);\n            return -1;\n        }\n        b->cached_value = malloc((size_t)len + 1);\n        if (!b->cached_value) {\n            *err = err_fmt(\"oom\");\n            fclose(fp);\n            return -1;\n        }\n        size_t got = fread(b->cached_value, 1, (size_t)len, fp);\n        fclose(fp);\n        b->cached_value[got] = '\\0';\n        b->cached_size = (int)got;\n    }\n    return 0;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ build_read_predecessor_lists() — issue 244\n *\n * For every call / write box, walk each input port and collect the\n * indices of any BOX_READ producers feeding it. The list lives on\n * the consumer's box record so dispatch can pull on demand without\n * a full graph scan. Reuses scan_input_feeders only indirectly —\n * we need the producer indices, not just counts, and the existing\n * helper doesn't expose them. The walk is small enough that an\n * inline loop is clearer than threading a callback through. */\nstatic int build_read_predecessor_lists(graph_t *g, char **err)\n{\n    for (int i = 0; i < g->n_boxes; i++) {\n        box_t *b = &g->boxes[i];\n        if (b->kind != BOX_CALL && b->kind != BOX_WRITE) continue;\n        if (b->n_inputs <= 0) continue;\n\n        b->n_read_predecessors    = calloc((size_t)b->n_inputs, sizeof(int));\n        b->read_predecessor_ids   = calloc((size_t)b->n_inputs, sizeof(int *));\n        b->read_pred_counter_slot = malloc((size_t)b->n_inputs * sizeof(int));\n        if (!b->n_read_predecessors || !b->read_predecessor_ids ||\n            !b->read_pred_counter_slot) {\n            *err = err_fmt(\"oom\");\n            return -1;\n        }\n        for (int p = 0; p < b->n_inputs; p++) b->read_pred_counter_slot[p] = -1;\n\n        for (int port = 0; port < b->n_inputs; port++) {\n            /* First pass: count. */\n            int n = 0;\n            for (int p = 0; p < g->n_boxes; p++) {\n                const box_t *prod = &g->boxes[p];\n                if (prod->kind != BOX_READ) continue;\n                for (int k = 0; k < prod->n_connections; k++) {\n                    const connection_t *c = &prod->connections[k];\n                    if (c->to_box_idx == i && c->to_input_idx == port) n++;\n                }\n            }\n            if (n == 0) continue;\n\n            int *ids = malloc((size_t)n * sizeof(int));\n            if (!ids) { *err = err_fmt(\"oom\"); return -1; }\n\n            /* Second pass: fill. */\n            int idx = 0;\n            for (int p = 0; p < g->n_boxes; p++) {\n                const box_t *prod = &g->boxes[p];\n                if (prod->kind != BOX_READ) continue;\n                for (int k = 0; k < prod->n_connections; k++) {\n                    const connection_t *c = &prod->connections[k];\n                    if (c->to_box_idx == i && c->to_input_idx == port) {\n                        ids[idx++] = p;\n                    }\n                }\n            }\n            b->n_read_predecessors[port]  = n;\n            b->read_predecessor_ids[port] = ids;\n        }\n    }\n    return 0;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ detect_entry_boxes() — phase 6",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/src/010-graph-loader.c"
}

340	 * Buffer memory comes from the slot store's unified allocator;
341	 * each successful read places the chunk handle into buf_chunks[i]
342	 * and the data pointer into bufs[i]. The caller releases them
343	 * via release_inputs on every exit path, whether success or
344	 * failure, so the chunks return to the allocator's free-lists. */
345	static int read_inputs(const dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, const box_t *b,
346	                       char **bufs, ua_chunk_t **buf_chunks,
347	                       const void **datas, int *sizes,
348	                       int *input_native,
349	                       int per_buf_cap, int *failed)
350	{
351	    *failed = 0;
352	    if (b->n_inputs == 0 || !b->input_slot_ids) return 0;
353	    ua_t *heap = slot_store_allocator(ctx->slots);
354	    int n_present = 0;
355	    for (int i = 0; i < b->n_inputs; i++) {
356	        buf_chunks[i] = ua_alloc(heap, (size_t)per_buf_cap);
357	        if (!buf_chunks[i]) { *failed = 1; return n_present; }
358	        bufs[i] = ua_data(buf_chunks[i]);
359	        int got;
360	        int mode = b->input_slot_modes ? b->input_slot_modes[i] : SLOT_MODE_PEEK;
361	        /* Slice 3 of issue 312: dual-ring slots are read via
362	         * slot_pop_ordered so the ordering ring drives the read
363	         * order across the native and JSON sub-rings. The returned
364	         * which_ring tag becomes the per-input native flag the spec
365	         * sees. Single-ring slots fall back to the existing
366	         * peek / pop and take their format from the box's per-port
367	         * input_edge_native[] classification (uniform across the
368	         * port — every cell in a single-ring slot is the same
369	         * format because the producers were all classified the
370	         * same way at graph load). */
371	        int32_t flags  = slot_flags(ctx->slots, b->input_slot_ids[i]);
372	        int     native = 1;
373	        if (flags >= 0 && (flags & SLOT_FLAG_DUAL_RING)) {
374	            int32_t which = SLOT_RING_NATIVE;
375	            got = slot_pop_ordered(ctx->slots, b->input_slot_ids[i],
376	                                   bufs[i], per_buf_cap, &which);
377	            native = (which == SLOT_RING_NATIVE) ? 1 : 0;
378	        } else {
379	            if (mode == SLOT_MODE_POP) {
380	                got = slot_pop(ctx->slots, b->input_slot_ids[i],
381	                               bufs[i], per_buf_cap);
382	            } else {
383	                got = slot_peek(ctx->slots, b->input_slot_ids[i],
384	                                bufs[i], per_buf_cap);
385	            }
386	            native = (b->input_edge_native && b->input_edge_native[i]) ? 1 : 0;
387	        }
388	        if (got < 0) {
389	            ua_unref(heap, buf_chunks[i]);
390	            buf_chunks[i] = NULL;
391	            bufs[i] = NULL;
392	            if (!b->inputs[i].optional) { *failed = 1; return n_present; }
393	            continue;
394	        }
395	        datas[n_present]        = bufs[i];
396	        sizes[n_present]        = got;
397	        if (input_native) input_native[n_present] = native;
398	        n_present++;
399	    }
400	    return n_present;
401	}
402	/* }}} */
403	
404	/* {{{ wire_is_native() — does this specific wire stay within one language?
405	 *
406	 * Returns 1 iff the given outgoing connection terminates at a
407	 * consumer in the producer's own language. Used by push_one_connection
408	 * to pick the dual-ring slot's native or JSON ring per wire — each
409	 * wire's writing style is its own. */
410	static int wire_is_native(const box_t *producer, const connection_t *c)
411	{
412	    if (!producer->output_edge_native || !producer->connections) return 0;
413	    int idx = (int)(c - producer->connections);
414	    if (idx < 0 || idx >= producer->n_connections) return 0;
415	    return producer->output_edge_native[idx];
416	}
417	/* }}} */
418	
419	/* {{{ all_wires_native() — does every outgoing wire stay in this language?
420	 *
421	 * Used to pass `output_native` to the spec as a hint about whether
422	 * any cross-language consumer exists. Specs that can produce
423	 * different formats may use this to pre-emptively pick the cheaper
424	 * one when all consumers are same-lang. Slice 4 of issue 312
425	 * threads this through; per-wire conversion (the symmetric
426	 * per-edge picture, where each wire gets its own format) is the
427	 * dispatch's job, not the spec's. */
428	static int all_wires_native(const box_t *b)
429	{
430	    if (!b->output_edge_native || b->n_connections <= 0) return 1;
431	    for (int j = 0; j < b->n_connections; j++) {
432	        if (!b->output_edge_native[j]) return 0;
433	    }
434	    return 1;
435	}

74	        free(err); return -1;
75	    }

81	    if (pool_init_barrier(rt->pool) != 0) return -1;
82	    return 0;
83	}

87	    if (rt->pool) pool_destroy(rt->pool);

### [927] type=attachment
ts: 2026-05-22T19:12:52.341Z

### [928] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T19:13:06.738Z

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
Notice what changed about the model — and what didn't. The old model said "read boxes are producers that push their value at startup, then sit idle." The new model says "read boxes are value sources that consumers pull from when they need a value." Same observable behavior in the common case (consumer fires once, sees its read box's value), but the *direction of causality* flips: push becomes pull. That flip removes a whole category of startup-ordering questions ("did the read box push before any consumer's first attempt?") and unlocks a category of new shapes (multi-spawn consumers cycling through multiple defaults).

The implementation trick was that the change didn't need a brand-new dispatch primitive — the existing slot-store atomic-counter (originally built for iterator/randomizer/weighted routing) handled the rotation. Reusing primitives across phases is the cheapest way to add semantics. Spec the contract abstractly enough at the bottom of the stack and the top layer's "new feature" turns into "two new lines using two existing tools."

Also worth flagging: I deliberately kept the pool runner's outputs report showing read-box cached values. Without that, every existing integration test would have broken on its substring match, and the "fix" would have been to update the tests — but those substring checks were proxying for "did this value reach the run?" which is still a legitimate question. Surfacing the cached value preserves that observable signal even though the underlying mechanism is now lazy.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

244 is committed and stays in `issues/` as "in progress" — the pull-on-demand model is fully wired end-to-end for the single-predecessor case, the rotation code path is written and loader-tested, but the integration fixture that exercises rotation across firing consumers is the small remaining piece. Honest scope: the code is done, one test is owed.

Ready for **312** next?

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
You just caught the same family of mistake twice in a row — the 317 commit message that conflated issue-scope with architectural coupling, and the 244 pool-runner branch that conflated "old test passes" with "user-visible behavior preserved." The shape is the same: I reached for a small bridge to keep something familiar working, and that bridge was telling a small lie about the new model. The right move both times was to do the harder thing — change the framing of the message, or change what the tests assert — instead of softening the reality.

The general rule that's emerging: when a redesign changes the *nature* of a thing (a task becomes not-a-task, a coupling becomes independence), the user-visible reports and the tests should also change shape to reflect that. Carrying the old surface forward looks like backward compatibility, but it's actually a quiet refusal to commit to the new design. Tests in particular should assert what the user cares about — values flowing — not which internal mechanism transported them.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

The hello run now reports one task, one output. The tests assert the consumer's result instead of the read box's pseudo-output. Ready for **312**?

{
  "content":"# Strategem — commit messages describe the runtime, not the issue\n\n## The pattern\n\nA commit message that uses scope-of-issue language (\"these features\nbelong together,\" \"this isn't done until both ship\") gets re-read\nlater as architecture language (\"these features are coupled,\" \"these\ntwo things are not independently deployable\"). Future readers cannot\ntell the difference between the two; they only see the commit text.\nThe lie compounds because no one re-reads issue files for archaeology\n— they re-read `git log`.\n\nWhere this kept showing up: the 317 commit text said the C and Bash\nbridges \"live or die together,\" which was true about the *issue\ncontract* (issue 317 said every spec must implement both directions)\nbut false about the *architecture* (the C bridge ships independently\nof the Bash bridge; each spec is its own .so dlopened in isolation;\nadding a Python spec doesn't touch either of them). A future reader\nlooking at the log entry would conclude the system is more entangled\nthan it is, and might design around a coupling that doesn't exist.\n\n## How to apply\n\nWhen writing a commit message:\n\n- Describe what the *runtime* now does — the artifact, the code path,\n  the behavior the user / next maintainer can observe.\n- Do not editorialise on top of the issue's bookkeeping. The fact\n  that an issue says \"every spec must X\" doesn't make the X-es\n  coupled; it makes them in-scope for the issue.\n- If multiple files / specs ship in one commit, describe each as its\n  own change, even when they share a motivation. \"Spec A and spec B\n  both gain X\" is honest; \"X for A and B is one indivisible unit\" is\n  not.\n- The shape of the commit (one or many) is a presentation choice. The\n  shape of the *system* is a property of the code. Don't conflate them.\n\n## Why it's worth a strategem and not just a habit\n\nBecause when redesigns happen six months later, this is the trap\nthe team falls into: someone reads the log, decides the coupling\nmust be real, designs around it, and the system stays falsely\nentangled because nobody had a reason to check whether the coupling\nwas load-bearing. The strategem catches it at commit time, where the\ncost of writing two sentences honestly is much less than the cost of\nthe false belief propagating.\n",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/strategems/commit-language-is-runtime-language.md"
}
```

{
  "content":"# Strategem — when a redesign changes the nature, change the surface\n\n## The pattern\n\nWhen a redesign changes the *nature* of a thing (a task becomes\nnot-a-task, a coupling becomes independence, a push becomes a pull,\na synchronous call becomes async), the user-visible reports and the\ntests must also change shape to reflect that. Carrying the old\nsurface forward looks like back-compat, but it's actually a quiet\nrefusal to commit to the new design.\n\nThe classic tell: a redesign ships, an existing test fails, and the\ninstinct is to add a small branch in the new code that makes the old\ntest pass. That branch is the lie. It says \"the new model is the new\nmodel, except in the report, where it pretends to be the old model so\nyour tests stay green.\"\n\nWhere this kept showing up: under 244, read boxes stopped running as\ntasks. The pool runner's `outputs:` report used to list every box's\ncaptured output, with read boxes producing their value. The first\n244 commit kept that report shape — added a branch that, when the\nbox was a read box with no captured output, surfaced its cached value\ninstead. Three integration tests with substring assertions like\n`\"who → World\"` kept passing because the report kept pretending the\nread box \"ran.\"\n\nThe fix was not the branch. The fix was: read boxes don't run, so\nthey don't appear in the outputs report; the tests update to assert\nthe consumer's output (which is what they were really proxying for\n— \"did the value reach its destination\") instead of the read box's\npseudo-output. The report now reflects reality and the tests now\nassert what users care about.\n\n## How to apply\n\nWhen a redesign lands and an existing test breaks:\n\n- First, look at what the test was asserting. Was it checking\n  user-visible behavior, or was it checking an implementation surface\n  that no longer exists?\n- If the assertion was a proxy for something deeper (\"did the value\n  flow through?\"), find the place in the new model where that\n  something is observable, and assert there.\n- If the assertion was just naming an internal mechanism that has\n  gone away, delete the assertion — the new model is the contract\n  now.\n- Don't add a branch in production code whose only purpose is to\n  keep an old test green. That's the smell.\n\nIf you find yourself writing a comment like \"preserves observability\nfor tests that named the old box id,\" stop and look harder. The\ntests are usually the wrong audience for that preservation; the user\nis.\n\n## Adjacent pattern — softening reality\n\nThere is a softer version of this trap that doesn't involve tests:\nadding a back-compat shim in user-facing output (\"we'll still print\nthe old line so people parsing logs by hand don't have to update\").\nThis is sometimes the right call, but more often it's the same\nrefusal-to-commit dressed up as kindness. If the old line was a lie\nunder the new model, printing it still is a lie. The graceful path\nis usually: print the truth, document the change, let downstream\nadapt — once. Carrying both forever is the cost of having lied once,\nand that cost compounds.\n",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/strategems/redesigns-change-the-surface-too.md"
}
```

```
1	# 304 — Task dispatch layer (C, replaces synchronous executor)
2	
3	## Status
4	open · design rewritten 2026-05-20 to the attempt-task model.
5	Current code is the older two-phase model (inline
6	spawn-on-input-ready check inside producer's dispatch action).
7	The refactor lands with issue 302's slab-allocator + refcount
8	work, since the two are intertwined.
9	
10	## Current behavior
11	The synchronous executor at `src/004-executor.lua` walks the graph in
12	dependency order, calling each box's driver, capturing stdout, decoding
13	JSON, and propagating values to downstream boxes. Single-threaded,
14	blocking, runs in Lua coroutines.
15	
16	## Concept
17	
18	The task dispatch layer is the C component every pool worker runs.
19	Every task in the pool is an **attempt task** — same shape, same
20	allocation, same call site. An attempt task either advances the
21	graph by one box invocation (when its consumer's inputs are
22	ready) or returns without doing anything (when they aren't).
23	
24	There is no separate "readiness check" task kind. There is no
25	"work task" kind distinct from a check. There is one kind, the
26	attempt; the work it performs is conditional on what it finds.
27	
28	The architecture doc's "structural shell" section frames this:
29	one allocator per kind of operation, one scheduler (the pool),
30	one task shape. The dispatch action is the one place the runtime:
31	
32	- Reads from input ring buffers (or pulls from read-box predecessors)
33	- Calls the language spec's `invoke`
34	- Picks the output branch per `routing.kind`
35	- Pushes the output value into every downstream consumer slot
36	- Submits attempt tasks for the consumers whose input sets may
37	  have just become satisfiable
38	
39	`004-executor.lua` is replaced wholesale. There is no Lua-level
40	executor in phase 3.
41	
42	## Task struct (cell layout)
43	
44	A task struct is a 64-byte slab cell. The layout reserves space
45	for every field every attempt may need; the fields' meaning is
46	the same for "this attempt found ready inputs and ran" and "this
47	attempt didn't find ready inputs and returned." One shape, two
48	outcomes.
49	
50	```c
```

```
208	## Routing kinds (issue 233)
209	
210	Every call box runs its function. Routing kinds differ only in
211	how the function's output is dispatched to downstream
212	connections:
213	
214	| `routing.kind`   | Output ports        | Branch picker                                                |
215	|------------------|---------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|
216	| (absent / plain) | single port         | fan to all outgoing connections                              |
217	| `comparator`     | `lt` / `eq` / `gt`  | compare(output, comparand) → lt/eq/gt                        |
218	| `iterator`       | `out_0` … `out_N-1` | `slot_read_inc(counter_slot, N)`                             |
219	| `randomizer`     | `out_0` … `out_N-1` | `hash(slot_read_inc(counter_slot, MAX)) % N`                 |
220	| `weighted`       | `out_0` … `out_N-1` | cumulative-band lookup against a counter scaled to PRECISION |
221	| `distributor`    | `out_0` … `out_N-1` | argmin over downstream slot fill levels                      |
222	
223	Plain call (no `routing` field) skips the branch picker entirely
224	— phase 5 fans the output to all wires. The function still runs
225	the same as for any branched call. Issue 233 carries the schema
226	and the per-kind UI.
227	
228	## Open questions
229	
230	(none currently — earlier questions resolved as follows:)
231	
232	- Output buffer sizing: heap-allocated to the box's declared output
233	  size at runtime. No fixed cap. Variable-size outputs go through the
234	  large-value heap (issue 302).
235	- Queued-input slot lifetime: producers hold refs while alive,
236	  consumer holds a ref while consuming, slot freed when all drop.
237	  References are the producer-liveness tracking mechanism.
238	- Comparator output encoding: dispatch layer handles comparison
239	  semantics directly. No spec involvement, no encoded "branch tag" in
240	  the slot — the branch is a routing-time value computed by the
241	  dispatch layer from the input.
242	
243	## Suggested implementation sequence
244	
245	1. Define `dispatch_task_t` and the `dispatch_action` skeleton.
246	2. Implement phase 1 (input check + ACT_BLOCK on slot wait list).
247	3. Implement phase 2 (input read into heap-allocated buffers).
248	4. Implement phase 4 (invoke for plain call boxes).
249	5. Implement phase 5 (output write via slot_push).
250	6. Implement phase 6 (unref consumed single-value inputs).
251	7. Smoke test: `hello` map (one plain call box, no routing) end-to-end
252	   through the pool. Assert the output slot contains the expected
253	   value.
254	8. Implement plain-call fan-out firing (phase 7, plain case).
255	9. Implement comparator routing (phase 3 + phase 7 comparator case).
256	   Test `branch-test` map.
257	10. Implement iterator routing and self-respawn (phase 3 + phase 7
258	    iterator case). Test an iterator map.
259	11. Wire up entry-box initial submission in the pool runner.
260	
261	## Relevant files
262	
263	- `src/004-executor.lua` — synchronous executor that this layer replaces
264	- `issues/301-pool-lifecycle-and-worker-init.md` — pool that runs this action
265	- `issues/302-wire-value-slot-store.md` — slot API used here
266	- `issues/303-language-runtime-spec.md` — `invoke` interface called here
267	- `issues/221-iterator-box.md` — iterator routing and self-rescheduling
268	- `issues/completed/108-branch-box-and-predicate-routing.md` — comparator routing
269	- `issues/213-queued-inputs-and-task-model.md` — queued-input slot semantics
270	
271	## Implementation log
272	
273	### Skeleton — 2026-05-12
274	
275	`src/012-dispatch.{c,h,info.md}` ships the types and the action's
276	signature with a stub body that increments a diagnostic counter
277	on the context and frees the task. `dispatch_ctx_t` bundles the
278	graph, slot store, spec registry, and pool — every task carries a
279	pointer to the context so the action has everything it needs
280	without globals. `dispatch_spawn(ctx, box_id, priority)`
281	allocates a task and calls `pool_spawn`.
282	
283	Two skeleton tests in `tests/012-dispatch-test.c` build the full
284	runtime (graph + slots + registry + pool) and submit either one
285	task per box, or 200 tasks at varied priorities, and assert that
286	the counter advances by the right amount.
287	
288	What's still ahead inside 304:
289	- Reading inputs from per-port slots (peek vs pop based on

295	  aren't routed through the worker context.
296	- Picking the output branch per `routing.kind` and pushing to
297	  consumer slots; this is the routing-table dispatch from the
298	  unified schema in issue 233.
299	- Spawn-on-input-ready chaining: each push to a consumer slot
300	  triggers a check, which may spawn the consumer's next task.
301	
302	### Plain-call dispatch + spawn-on-ready — 2026-05-12

306	2. Read every input port via `slot_peek` into a heap buffer.
307	3. Dispatch on `box->kind`:

430	### Cell-tagged ordering across parallel iterator workers — 2026-05-19
431	
432	Earlier multi-spawn slot pushes carried `tag = 0`; pop order was
433	fill order (which is FIFO under the per-slot spinlock but
434	non-deterministic across producer interleaving).
435	
436	Tag propagation is now wired through:
437	
438	- `push_one_connection` / `push_branch` take an explicit `tag`
439	  argument forwarded to `slot_push`.
440	- Iterator routing passes the freshly-incremented counter as
441	  the tag; randomizer and weighted routing pass their counter
442	  snapshot so any downstream tagged slot still serves in stable
443	  order; plain/comparator pushes pass `0`.
444	- `graph_attach_runtime` sets `SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED` on every
445	  multi-spawn box's input slots. The slot store's tagged-pop
446	  rule (lowest tag first; FIFO among ties via filled-index
447	  scan) means that even when two iterator tasks run on
448	  different workers in opposite order, the downstream consumer
449	  reads them in invocation order.
450	
451	Verified by re-running all 6 fixture maps and the dedicated
452	`test_iterator_multi_fire` test (which now exercises tagged
453	input slots).
454	
455	### Design rewrite to the attempt-task model — 2026-05-20
456	
457	What changed in the design (not yet in the code):
458	
459	- The two-phase model (producer's dispatch action calls
460	  `dispatch_spawn_if_ready` inline on each consumer; if ready,
461	  spawns a fresh `dispatch_task_t` for the consumer) is replaced
462	  by the **attempt-task model**. Every task in the pool is an
463	  attempt; the work happens conditionally when the attempt finds
464	  inputs ready. One allocation per invocation, not two.
465	- The task struct grows from `{ box_id }` to the 64-byte cell
466	  layout above (free-list link, box pointer, task_id, flags,
467	  input snapshot, output destinations, live_wire_count
468	  placeholder).
469	- Read-box producers stop pushing at startup; they become
470	  pull-on-demand sources per issue 244.
471	- The "no parking" rule from the structural-shell doc holds: an
472	  attempt that finds its inputs unsatisfied returns and is
473	  collected; the next push to the consumer triggers a fresh
474	  attempt.
475	
476	What's still in the code from the older model:
477	
478	- The `dispatch_action` body in `src/012-dispatch.c` is the
479	  two-phase shape: read inputs → invoke → push → inline
480	  `dispatch_spawn_if_ready` per consumer. Plain, comparator, and
481	  iterator routing all work under this shape.
482	- `dispatch_task_t` is still `{ box_id, task_id }` plus a
483	  context pointer.
484	- Read boxes still push at startup.
485	
486	The refactor lands with issue 302's slab-allocator + LVH
487	refcount work, since the task-cell layout and the refcount
488	plumbing share the same allocator changes. After 302 ships:
489	
490	1. `src/012-dispatch.c::dispatch_action` becomes the single
491	   attempt-loop body.
492	2. Task allocation moves from per-spawn malloc to slab pop.
493	3. Spawn logic moves from "producer inlines check" to "producer
494	   submits attempts" — one submission per distinct downstream
495	   consumer box, at the END of the producer.
496	4. Read-box predecessors are recognized at attempt time and
497	   pulled round-robin per 244.
498	5. The `live_wire_count` field on the cell is populated by
499	   graph load; the box-retirement consumer is the stretch
```

42	## Task struct (cell layout)
43	
44	A task struct is a 64-byte slab cell. The layout reserves space
45	for every field every attempt may need; the fields' meaning is
46	the same for "this attempt found ready inputs and ran" and "this
47	attempt didn't find ready inputs and returned." One shape, two
48	outcomes.
49	
50	```c
51	typedef struct {
52	    void   *next_free;          /* 8B — free-list link, overwritten on alloc */
53	    box_t  *box;                /* 8B — the box this attempt is for */
54	    uint32_t task_id;           /* 4B — event-log correlation (311) */
55	    uint32_t flags;             /* 4B — work / attempt / priority hint */
56	    void   *output_destinations;/* 8B — per-wire consumer slot pointers */
57	    void   *input_snapshot;     /* 8B — values OR pointer to values-array */
58	    uint32_t live_wire_count;   /* 4B — box-refcount slot (244 / retirement) */
59	    uint32_t _pad;              /* 4B — alignment */
60	    /* remaining 16B available for inline input snapshot or extensions */
61	} attempt_task_t;
62	```
63	
64	The `live_wire_count` slot is the documented placeholder for the
65	box-level retirement system. The dispatch action populates it at
66	creation; the box-retirement consumer that DOES something with
67	the count is the stretch goal in 302. Until that ships, the slot
68	is honest but unread — its presence is documentation that the
69	field belongs in the design.
70	
71	The `input_snapshot` is the FIFO-popped values from each input
72	slot's ring buffer (or the pointer to a read-box's value when
73	pulled per 244). Once the attempt has snapshotted, the slot
74	state is independent of the task's execution.
75	
76	The `output_destinations` is the array of consumer slot pointers
77	the attempt will write to — one per outgoing wire (or one per
78	firing branch, for routed boxes). Pre-computed at graph load and
79	referenced through the box pointer.
80	
81	## The dispatch action — the attempt loop
82	
83	```
84	producer task (a completing attempt that ran):
85	   compute output via spec invoke
86	   for each downstream consumer box C this producer just touched:
87	      write all values to C's input slots
88	         (snapshotted into the attempt's cell at the END)
89	   for each distinct C:
90	      slab_alloc() → attempt_task for C
91	      pool_spawn(attempt_task)
92	   decrement input $ref counts
93	   decrement upstream boxes' live_predecessor_count if applicable
94	   slab_free(this cell)
95	
96	attempt task (newly picked up by a worker):
97	   if every required input on C is satisfiable
98	      (slot value or data-box pull per 244):
99	      snapshot values into local vars
100	      invoke spec, get output
101	      [continue as producer task above]
102	   slab_free(this cell)
103	```
104	
105	Three properties of this loop:
106	
107	- **Targeted attempts.** A producer knows which consumer boxes it
108	  just pushed to; it submits attempts for *those specific
109	  consumers*, not a generic re-evaluation.
110	- **One attempt per (producer, consumer-box) pair.** Even if a
111	  producer writes to multiple slots on the same consumer, only
112	  one attempt is submitted for that consumer. Dedup happens at
113	  the end of the producer's run, after every output value has
114	  been placed. The attempt is guaranteed to find a coherent
115	  input set, not a half-written one.
116	- **No parking.** An attempt that finds its inputs unsatisfied
117	  returns. The shell does not hold tasks in waiting states. The
118	  next producer push to any of the consumer's input slots
119	  triggers another attempt with the same shape. Eventually one
120	  of those attempts finds the input set complete and runs the
121	  work.
122	
123	The attempt task's "if inputs ready, run" branch IS the
124	inline-fast-path for same-language chains. There is no separate
125	spawn step between the readiness decision and the work; the work
126	just happens, in the same allocation, on the same worker. A
127	producer pushing into a fast same-language consumer fires an
128	attempt that runs immediately on whichever worker picks it up —
129	one allocation, one queue round-trip per box invocation.
130	
131	## Initial submission
132	
133	At startup the pool runner walks the graph and submits an attempt
134	task for each **entry box** (a box whose required inputs are all
135	satisfiable from literal values or read-box predecessors, i.e.
136	whose attempt would succeed on first try). Read boxes do NOT
137	push at startup under 244 — they're pulled on demand from inside
138	attempts that find their slots empty and their port has a
139	read-box predecessor.
140	
141	Iterator counter slots start at 0. Literal-only entry boxes
142	submit their first attempt at startup and increment from there.
143	
144	## Iterator counter and parallel iteration
145	
146	The iterator's counter lives in a `SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER` slot
147	allocated for the box at graph load time (issue 302). The
148	dispatch action reads and increments it atomically, mid-action:
149	
150	```c
151	uint32_t branch_idx = slot_read_inc(box->counter_slot, n_iter_outputs);
152	slot_push(downstream[branch_idx].input_slot, output_buf, output_size,
153	          /* tag = */ branch_idx);
154	```
155	
156	`slot_read_inc` returns the current counter value `mod
157	n_iter_outputs` and atomically advances the underlying counter.
158	Concurrent iterator tasks on different workers each call
159	`slot_read_inc` independently and each get a unique value —
160	**iterator tasks parallelize across workers**, picking distinct
161	branches without coordination.
162	
163	No box-side mutable state, no per-task counter snapshot. The
164	counter is just data in a slot the dispatch layer reads.
165	
166	## Cross-iterator pairing under parallel iteration
167	
168	With parallel iterator tasks, push ordering at the consumer is
169	non-deterministic — task 6 of iterator A may push to consumer C
170	before task 5 if 6 happened to land on a faster worker. FIFO pop
171	at C would consume them out of iteration order.
172	
173	The fix is **counter-tagged pushes**. Each iterator push (in phase
174	5) carries the counter value the action read for this invocation:
175	
176	```c
```

585	/* }}} */
586	
587	/* {{{ push_routed() — branch picker over routing.kind, then push */
588	static int push_routed(dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, const box_t *b,
589	                       const void *out_bytes, int out_size)
590	{
591	    /* Read and write boxes don't carry a routing kind; their
592	     * output fans plain. Same for call boxes with no routing set
593	     * (treated as plain). */
594	    if (b->kind != BOX_CALL) return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
595	
596	    switch (b->routing.kind) {
597	        case ROUTING_PLAIN:
598	            return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
599	
600	        case ROUTING_COMPARATOR: {
601	            double v = parse_double(out_bytes, out_size);
602	            const char *branch =
603	                (v < b->routing.comparand) ? "lt" :
604	                (v > b->routing.comparand) ? "gt" : "eq";
605	            return push_branch(ctx, b, branch, out_bytes, out_size, 0) < 0 ? -1 : 0;
606	        }
607	
608	        case ROUTING_ITERATOR: {
609	            if (b->counter_slot_id < 0 || b->routing.n_outputs < 1) {
610	                /* No counter slot or zero outputs — fall back to plain. */
611	                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
612	            }
613	            /* The counter is both the branch selector AND the order
614	             * tag for the consumer slot (issue 304 cell-tagged
615	             * ordering). Tagged consumers serve pops by ascending
616	             * tag, so parallel iterator tasks land in invocation
617	             * order regardless of which worker finishes first. */
618	            uint32_t idx = slot_read_inc(ctx->slots, b->counter_slot_id,
619	                                         (uint32_t)b->routing.n_outputs);
620	            char branch[24];
621	            snprintf(branch, sizeof branch, "out_%u", idx);
622	            return push_branch(ctx, b, branch, out_bytes, out_size, idx) < 0 ? -1 : 0;
623	        }
624	
625	        case ROUTING_RANDOMIZER: {
626	            if (b->counter_slot_id < 0 || b->routing.n_outputs < 1) {
627	                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
628	            }
629	            /* Mix the monotonic counter so consecutive invocations
630	             * don't go to consecutive branches. xorshift32-style,
631	             * no crypto pretensions. */
632	            uint32_t i = slot_read_inc(ctx->slots, b->counter_slot_id, UINT32_MAX);
633	            uint32_t h = i;
634	            h ^= h >> 16; h *= 0x85ebca6bu;
635	            h ^= h >> 13; h *= 0xc2b2ae35u;
636	            h ^= h >> 16;
637	            uint32_t branch_idx = h % (uint32_t)b->routing.n_outputs;
638	            char branch[24];
639	            snprintf(branch, sizeof branch, "out_%u", branch_idx);
640	            /* Randomizer is also multi-spawn-aware; pass i as tag so
641	             * any downstream tagged slot still serves in order. */
642	            return push_branch(ctx, b, branch, out_bytes, out_size, i) < 0 ? -1 : 0;
643	        }
644	
645	        case ROUTING_WEIGHTED: {
646	            if (b->counter_slot_id < 0 || b->routing.n_outputs < 1 ||
647	                !b->routing.weights) {
648	                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
649	            }
650	            /* Cumulative-band lookup against a counter scaled to a
651	             * fixed precision. The last band absorbs floating-point
652	             * rounding so the bands sum to the whole range. */
653	            enum { WEIGHTED_PRECISION = 1000 };
654	            uint32_t i = slot_read_inc(ctx->slots, b->counter_slot_id,
655	                                       (uint32_t)WEIGHTED_PRECISION);
656	            double total = 0.0;
657	            for (int k = 0; k < b->routing.n_outputs; k++) {
658	                total += b->routing.weights[k];
659	            }
660	            if (total <= 0.0) {
661	                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
662	            }
663	            int branch_idx = b->routing.n_outputs - 1;
664	            double acc = 0.0;
665	            for (int k = 0; k < b->routing.n_outputs; k++) {
666	                acc += (b->routing.weights[k] / total) * WEIGHTED_PRECISION;
667	                if ((double)i < acc) { branch_idx = k; break; }
668	            }
669	            char branch[24];
670	            snprintf(branch, sizeof branch, "out_%d", branch_idx);
671	            return push_branch(ctx, b, branch, out_bytes, out_size, i) < 0 ? -1 : 0;
672	        }
673	
674	        case ROUTING_DISTRIBUTOR:
675	        default:
676	            /* Distributor inspects downstream slot fill levels; not
677	             * yet implemented. Fall back to plain so the runtime
678	             * doesn't deadlock. */
679	            return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
680	    }
681	}
682	/* }}} */
683	
684	/* {{{ capture() — optional test hook, memcpy output into ctx */
685	static void capture(dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, int box_id,
686	                    const void *bytes, int size)
687	{
688	    if (!ctx->capture_outputs || !ctx->last_outputs) return;
689	    if (box_id < 0 || box_id >= ctx->n_boxes) return;
690	    free(ctx->last_outputs[box_id]);
691	    char *copy = malloc((size_t)size + 1);
692	    if (!copy) return;
693	    if (size > 0) memcpy(copy, bytes, (size_t)size);
694	    copy[size] = '\0';

/* Check whether the slot has any filled cell available for read.
 * Used by the dispatch layer's spawn-on-input-ready rule (issue 304).

 * Returns 1 if a value is available, 0 otherwise. For ATOMIC_COUNTER
 * slots, always returns 1 (the counter is always readable). */

/* }}} */

int32_t       slot_store_size(slot_store_t *s);

 * variant per slot. Returns the flag mask on success, -1 on invalid
 * slot id. */
int32_t       slot_flags(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id);

Ties resolve via\n             * the counter slot so a steady stream still rotates\n             * across equally-empty branches instead of always\n             * picking the same low-index one. */\n            if (b->counter_slot_id < 0 || b->routing.n_outputs < 1) {\n                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);\n            }\n            int best_branch  = -1;\n            int32_t best_max = INT32_MAX;\n            for (int k = 0; k < b->routing.n_outputs; k++) {\n                char want[24];\n                snprintf(want, sizeof want, \"out_%d\", k);\n                int32_t this_max = 0;\n                int found_any = 0;\n                for (int j = 0; j < b->n_connections; j++) {\n                    const connection_t *c = &b->connections[j];\n                    if (!c->from_branch ||\n                        strcmp(c->from_branch, want) != 0) continue;\n                    int dst = c->to_box_idx;\n                    if (dst < 0) continue;\n                    const box_t *db = graph_box(ctx->graph, dst);\n                    int port = c->to_input_idx;\n                    if (!db || port < 0 || port >= db->n_inputs ||\n                        !db->input_slot_ids) continue;\n                    int32_t fill = slot_fill_count(ctx->slots,\n                                                   db->input_slot_ids[port]);\n                    if (fill > this_max) this_max = fill;\n                    found_any = 1;\n                }\n                if (!found_any) continue;\n                if (this_max < best_max) {\n                    best_max    = this_max;\n                    best_branch = k;\n                }\n            }\n            if (best_branch < 0) {\n                /* No outgoing connection on any branch — falling\n                 * back to plain still pushes nothing useful, but\n                 * keeps the action's contract (return 0 means OK). */\n                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);\n            }\n            char branch[24];\n            snprintf(branch, sizeof branch, \"out_%d\", best_branch);\n            uint32_t tag = slot_read_inc(ctx->slots, b->counter_slot_id,\n                                         UINT32_MAX);\n            return push_branch(ctx, b, branch, out_bytes, out_size, tag) < 0\n                       ? -1 : 0;\n        }\n\n        default:\n            /* Unknown routing kind — push plain as the safe default. */\n            return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);\n    }\n}",

  "new_string":"/* {{{ test_distributor_picks_least_full() — issue 304 */\n/* Build a temp map: one distributor `dist` (n_outputs=2) wired to\n * two echo sinks `s0` / `s1`. The distributor's argmin-over-fill picker should\n * route to `s0` (less loaded). Only `s0` captures an output —\n * `s1` was never spawn-triggered.\n *\n * Counter-rotated tie-breaking is also exercised here implicitly:\n * if the picker had a bias toward branch 0 the test would pass\n * spuriously, so the inverse case (s0 pre-filled, expect s1) is\n * the actual proof. We run both. */\nstatic int build_distributor_fixture(char *dir, size_t cap)\n{\n    char tmpl[] = \"/tmp/soramech-distributor-XXXXXX\";\n    char *got = mkdtemp(tmpl);\n    if (!got) return -1;\n    snprintf(dir, cap, \"%s\", got);\n\n    char p[4096];\n    snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/meta.json\", dir);\n    FILE *fp = fopen(p, \"w\");\n    if (!fp) return -1;\n    fputs(\"{\\\"name\\\":\\\"dist\\\",\\\"entry_box_id\\\":\\\"dist\\\",\"\n          \"\\\"src_dirs\\\":[\\\"tests/maps/calc/src\\\"]}\", fp);\n    fclose(fp);\n\n    snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/boxes\", dir);\n    mkdir(p, 0755);\n\n    snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/boxes/dist.json\", dir);\n    fp = fopen(p, \"w\");\n    fputs(\"{\\\"id\\\":\\\"dist\\\",\\\"kind\\\":\\\"call\\\",\\\"lang\\\":\\\"lua\\\",\"\n          \"\\\"ref\\\":\\\"src/calc.lua\\\",\\\"fn\\\":\\\"add\\\",\"\n          \"\\\"inputs\\\":[\"\n              \"{\\\"name\\\":\\\"a\\\",\\\"type\\\":\\\"string\\\",\\\"value\\\":\\\"1\\\"},\"\n              \"{\\\"name\\\":\\\"b\\\",\\\"type\\\":\\\"string\\\",\\\"value\\\":\\\"0\\\"}\"\n          \"],\"\n          \"\\\"routing\\\":{\\\"kind\\\":\\\"distributor\\\",\\\"n_outputs\\\":2},\"\n          \"\\\"connections\\\":[\"\n              \"{\\\"from_box\\\":\\\"dist\\\",\\\"from_branch\\\":\\\"out_0\\\",\"\n                \"\\\"to_box\\\":\\\"s0\\\",\\\"to_input\\\":\\\"x\\\"},\"\n              \"{\\\"from_box\\\":\\\"dist\\\",\\\"from_branch\\\":\\\"out_1\\\",\"\n                \"\\\"to_box\\\":\\\"s1\\\",\\\"to_input\\\":\\\"x\\\"}\"\n          \"]}\", fp);\n    fclose(fp);\n\n    for (int i = 0; i < 2; i++) {\n        snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/boxes/s%d.json\", dir, i);\n        fp = fopen(p, \"w\");\n        fprintf(fp,\n            \"{\\\"id\\\":\\\"s%d\\\",\\\"kind\\\":\\\"call\\\",\\\"lang\\\":\\\"lua\\\",\"\n            \"\\\"ref\\\":\\\"src/calc.lua\\\",\\\"fn\\\":\\\"identity\\\",\"\n            \"\\\"inputs\\\":[{\\\"name\\\":\\\"x\\\",\\\"type\\\":\\\"string\\\"}],\"\n            \"\\\"routing\\\":{\\\"kind\\\":\\\"plain\\\"}}\",\n            i);\n        fclose(fp);\n    }\n    return 0;\n}\n\nstatic void cleanup_distributor_fixture(const char *dir)\n{\n    char p[4096];\n    snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/boxes/dist.json\", dir); unlink(p);\n    snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/boxes/s0.json\",   dir); unlink(p);\n    snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/boxes/s1.json\",   dir); unlink(p);\n    snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/meta.json\",       dir); unlink(p);\n    snprintf(p, sizeof p, \"%s/boxes\",           dir); rmdir(p);\n    rmdir(dir);\n}\n\nstatic int distributor_case(const char *dir, int pre_fill_branch,\n                            int expect_picked)\n{\n    runtime_t rt;\n    if (runtime_setup(&rt, dir, 2, 1) != 0) {\n        runtime_teardown(&rt); return 0;\n    }\n    char *err = NULL;\n    ASSERT(dispatch_push_literals(&rt.ctx, &err) == 0);\n\n    int dist = graph_box_index(rt.graph, \"dist\");\n    int s0   = graph_box_index(rt.graph, \"s0\");\n    int s1   = graph_box_index(rt.graph, \"s1\");\n    int targets[2] = { s0, s1 };\n    ASSERT(dist >= 0 && s0 >= 0 && s1 >= 0);\n\n    const box_t *busy = graph_box(rt.graph, targets[pre_fill_branch]);\n    ASSERT(busy && busy->input_slot_ids);\n    /* Inflate the chosen sink's slot fill so the distributor sees\n     * it as \"loaded\" without ever spawn-triggering it. */\n    ASSERT(slot_push(rt.slots, busy->input_slot_ids[0], \"x\", 1, 0) == 0);\n\n    dispatch_spawn_if_ready(&rt.ctx, dist, 0);\n    pool_wait_quiescent(rt.pool);\n\n    int picked    = targets[expect_picked];\n    int rejected  = targets[1 - expect_picked];\n    ASSERT(rt.ctx.last_outputs[picked]   != NULL);\n    ASSERT(rt.ctx.last_outputs[rejected] == NULL);\n\n    runtime_teardown(&rt);\n    return 1;\n}\n\nstatic int test_distributor_picks_least_full(void)\n{\n    char dir[1024];\n    ASSERT(build_distributor_fixture(dir, sizeof dir) == 0);\n\n    /* s1 pre-filled → s0 is less loaded → distributor picks s0. */\n    int ok_a = distributor_case(dir, /*pre_fill=*/1, /*expect=*/0);\n    /* s0 pre-filled → distributor picks s1. */\n    int ok_b = distributor_case(dir, /*pre_fill=*/0, /*expect=*/1);\n\n    cleanup_distributor_fixture(dir);\n    ASSERT(ok_a && ok_b);\n    return 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ main() */\nint main(void)\n{",

{
  "old_string":"## Status\nopen · design rewritten 2026-05-20 to the attempt-task model.\nCurrent code is the older two-phase model (inline\nspawn-on-input-ready check inside producer's dispatch action).\nThe refactor lands with issue 302's slab-allocator + refcount\nwork, since the two are intertwined.\n\n## Current behavior\nThe synchronous executor at `src/004-executor.lua` walks the graph in\ndependency order, calling each box's driver, capturing stdout, decoding\nJSON, and propagating values to downstream boxes. Single-threaded,\nblocking, runs in Lua coroutines.",
  "replace_all":false,
  "new_string":"## Status\nin progress — every routing kind ships and every fixture passes\nend-to-end. The remaining piece, the attempt-task model rewrite\n(documented below), is a structural refactor with no observable\nbehavior change; it has its own scope and lands separately when\nprioritised. See \"Attempt-task refactor (future)\" near the end.\n\n## Current behavior\n`src/012-dispatch.c` ships the C dispatch layer. The action body\nreads each input port (peek for single-spawn boxes, pop for\nmulti-spawn ones; dual-ring for cross-language wires), invokes the\nresolved spec's `invoke` callback, picks the output branch per\n`routing.kind`, and pushes the output bytes to every outgoing\nconnection's consumer slot. Each push fires `dispatch_spawn_if_ready`\non the destination; consumers with all required inputs ready spawn\nas fresh tasks. Read boxes are pulled on demand by consumers, not\nspawned (issue 244).\n\nRouting kinds, all implemented:\n\n- `plain` — fan to every outgoing connection.\n- `comparator` — compare output to `comparand`, pick `lt`/`eq`/`gt`.\n- `iterator` — `slot_read_inc(counter, n_outputs)` picks the\n  branch; counter doubles as the per-cell tag so parallel iterator\n  tasks land in invocation order downstream.\n- `randomizer` — xorshift-mix the counter, modulo n_outputs.\n- `weighted` — cumulative-band lookup against counter scaled to a\n  fixed precision; ties absorbed by the last band.\n- `distributor` — argmin over downstream slot fill levels. Ties\n  resolve via counter-rotated iteration so a steady stream\n  spreads across equally-empty branches instead of always biasing\n  toward index 0.\n\nThe synchronous executor at `src/004-executor.lua` is wholly\nreplaced.",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/304-task-dispatch-layer.md"
}
```

Branches\nof equal load break ties via the routing counter slot — read once\nper push, used both as the iteration offset (so ties rotate in\nround-robin order instead of always biasing toward index 0) and\nas the tag carried on the push (so downstream tagged consumers\nstill serve in invocation order).\n\nA dispatch unit test builds an inline fixture with one distributor\nand two echo sinks, pre-fills one sink's slot to mark it loaded,\nspawns the distributor, and asserts the other sink received the\nvalue. The test runs the inverse case as well so a stuck index-0\nbias would fail the second half.\n\nThis completes every routing kind in the unified schema (issue\n233). The attempt-task structural refactor remains in 304's body\nas a documented follow-on; it has no observable behavior to add,\nso it doesn't block closing any of the consumer issues that depend\non dispatch features.\n\nCo-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>\nEOF\n)\"",

### [1149] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T19:29:08.242Z

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
There's a discipline at work in this commit that the earlier ones had to learn: the issue body was honest about what's left. Distributor was the genuine feature gap — six routing kinds in the schema, only five with code, fixing the sixth was bounded work. The attempt-task model rewrite is a different thing — a structural refactor with no observable behavior change. Conflating them would have either (a) pushed me to do a much larger commit "to close 304," or (b) let me handwave the rewrite as somehow already-done. Instead, the body now says "every routing kind ships, structural refactor still owed." Issue stays in progress, but the scope of what "in progress" means is precise.

The catch I almost made on the picker is also worth noting. My first pass had `if (this_max < best_max)` with `best_max = INT32_MAX` — correct for finding the minimum, but with equal fills the very first branch wins every time. That's the same "biased toward index 0" bug that distributor is supposed to fix at a higher level. The cure was to read the counter once and use it for *both* the iteration offset (so ties spread) *and* the push tag (so downstream order stays meaningful). One value serving two purposes — both of which were already needed — instead of a fresh source of entropy.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

304 stays in `issues/` as "in progress" — every routing kind ships and is tested end-to-end (8 dispatch tests including the new distributor case), and the attempt-task model rewrite is honestly scoped as a structural follow-on rather than scope-crept into this commit. Ready for **312** next?

### [1203] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T19:33:44.557Z

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
The trick that made this test honest — and the first attempt fail — is worth dwelling on. My first version asserted `seen[0] == "v0"` because that's the order I had typed the read boxes in. The filesystem disagreed: directory enumeration returned them in some other order, so the loader's predecessor list had a different order, so the counter walked them in a different order, and the assertion died. The fix wasn't to sort the list in the loader (which would have made the loader honor a property nobody had asked for). The fix was to ask the test the question it actually cares about: *"each k-th spawn picks the k-th entry in the loader's list,"* not *"each k-th spawn picks the k-th entry that I wrote on disk."*

The rotation invariant is a property of the dispatch layer (counter rotates through the list it was handed). The list order is a property of the loader (whatever filesystem returns, plus whatever de-duplication the loader does). Conflating them in the test would have either over-specified the loader's behavior or made the test flaky on different filesystems. Asking the right question — through the predecessor list — keeps each layer's contract independent. Same principle the strategem about "describe the runtime, not the paperwork" was getting at: name what's actually load-bearing in the thing you're testing, and let the other thing be free.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

244 closed, moved to `issues/completed/`. The dispatch suite is now at 9 tests including the new `read_predecessor_rotation`. Ready for **312** next?

1	# 312 — Same-language wire fast path: dual-ring slot, JSON at language borders
2	
3	## Status
4	substantively complete on the dual-ring foundation (2026-05-21);
5	**spec-contract simplification pending** — see the Amendment
6	section below. Dual-ring slots, per-edge classification, and the
7	per-cell ordering ring are in place and correct. The spec-side
8	shape needs one more pass to remove the per-input format flags
9	and the multiple invoke variants from `lang_spec_t`; that
10	simplification is the final slice of this issue.
11	
12	## Amendment (2026-05-22) — collapse to one `invoke`, native-only
13	
14	The original slice plan kept three invoke entry points
15	(`invoke`, `invoke_native`, `invoke_json`) and pushed per-input
16	format branching into each spec. After implementing through
17	slice 5 we realised the per-input branching is dispatch's job,
18	not the spec's. The revised target:
19	
20	- **Dispatch normalises every input to the consuming spec's
21	  native bytes before calling invoke.** When a popped cell is
22	  tagged `RING_JSON`, dispatch calls the consumer spec's
23	  `json_to_native` to convert the JSON bytes into the spec's
24	  native byte form (written into a temporary slot or directly
25	  into the input position dispatch hands to invoke). When the
26	  cell is tagged `RING_NATIVE`, dispatch passes the slot's bytes
27	  straight through — no conversion, the producer already wrote
28	  in the consumer's format because they share a language.
29	- **`invoke` sees one shape only**: a list of native-byte input
30	  pointers, and an output slot to write native bytes into. No
31	  `input_native[]` array, no `output_native` flag. The signature
32	  becomes:
33	
34	  ```c
35	  int invoke(handle, box, file_path, fn_name,
36	             const void **input_data, const int *input_sizes,
37	             int n_inputs,
38	             void *out_buf, int out_buf_capacity, int *out_size);
39	  ```
40	
41	- **`invoke_native` and `invoke_json` are deleted from
42	  `lang_spec_t`.** The struct keeps `invoke`,
43	  `native_to_json`, and `json_to_native` (plus `init`,
44	  `teardown`, `compile`, `name`, `file_ext`).
45	- **Cross-language output** is handled by dispatch after invoke:
46	  the producer writes native bytes to its output slot once;
47	  dispatch calls `native_to_json` once on those bytes if any
48	  outgoing connection's `output_edge_native[j]` is false, then
49	  pushes the JSON bytes to each cross-language consumer's
50	  `ring_json`. Same-language consumers receive the original
51	  native bytes directly.
52	- **No "bytes vs native" distinction in the contract.** "Native"
53	  means: bytes written in the format the spec emits and reads
54	  back. There is no separate `native_to_bytes` /
55	  `bytes_to_native` bridge; the spec's own native-write and
56	  native-read already cover same-language wires by writing the
57	  right bytes into the slot at push time and reading them out
58	  at pop time. The slot is the medium; the spec is the codec.
59	- **Why this collapses cleanly**: same-language wires never
60	  involve dispatch's bridges at all — producer writes native to
61	  the slot, consumer reads native from the slot, full stop.
62	  Cross-language wires call exactly one bridge: `native_to_json`
63	  on the producer side at push (so the JSON ring carries JSON),
64	  `json_to_native` on the consumer side at pop (so invoke sees
65	  native). The bridges fire once per language crossing, never
66	  twice, and never inside a same-language path.
67	
68	The result is one entry point, two bridges, and a dispatch layer
69	that owns all format-conversion timing. Specs no longer reason
70	about wire format at all.
71	
72	### Final-slice plan
73	
74	1. **Remove `input_native[]` and `output_native` from
75	   `lang_invoke_fn`** in `langs/lang-spec.h`. Update every spec
76	   stub.
77	2. **Move JSON → native decode out of the spec's `invoke` into
78	   dispatch's `read_inputs`** (`src/012-dispatch.c`). On a
79	   `RING_JSON` pop, dispatch calls the consumer spec's
80	   `json_to_native` and stages the resulting native bytes for
81	   invoke.
82	3. **Move JSON encoding for cross-language consumers out of the
83	   spec's `invoke` into dispatch's push path**
84	   (`src/012-dispatch.c`). After invoke returns, if any
85	   outgoing connection is cross-language, call `native_to_json`
86	   once and push the JSON bytes to each cross-language
87	   consumer.
88	4. **Delete `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` slots from
89	   `lang_spec_t`.** Drop the no-op stubs from each spec.
90	5. **Restore Lua's pre-slice-4 simple invoke** — it reads
91	   native bytes from inputs, runs the function, writes native
92	   bytes to the output. No per-input branch.
93	6. **Tests**: existing same-language and cross-language tests
94	   should pass unchanged; a new test covers a mixed-fan-in port
95	   where one cell arrives JSON-tagged (dispatch decodes before
96	   invoke) and one arrives native-tagged (dispatch passes
97	   through).
98	
99	## Current behavior
100	
```

40	 * spec interface stand-alone for callers that only need the typedef
41	 * names. */
42	typedef struct box box_t;
43	
44	/* {{{ Shared signatures */
45	/* Invoke signature — shared by `invoke`, `invoke_native`, and
46	 * `invoke_json`. The dispatch layer picks which one to call based
47	 * on the wire-format classification in issue 312.
48	 *
49	 * Per-input format flag (`input_native[i]`): non-zero iff the
50	 * bytes in `input_data[i]` are this spec's native form; zero iff
51	 * they are JSON from a cross-language producer. The spec
52	 * dispatches internally — its decoder for that input picks the
53	 * native or JSON path based on the flag.
54	 *
55	 * Per-output format flag (`output_native`): non-zero iff the
56	 * dispatch wants the spec to write native bytes to `out_buf`,
57	 * zero iff it wants JSON. The spec produces one form per call;
58	 * cross-language fan-out is handled by the dispatch layer, which
59	 * converts via `native_to_json` once and pushes the JSON to every
60	 * cross-language consumer.
61	 *
62	 * On non-dual-ring slots (single-language inputs or single-language
63	 * outputs only), the dispatch passes NULL for `input_native` and
64	 * 0 for `output_native`; the spec falls back to its default
65	 * decode (typically JSON, which Lua's existing bridge handles).
66	 * `input_native` may be NULL even when `n_inputs > 0` — specs
67	 * must check before dereferencing. */
68	typedef int (*lang_invoke_fn)(void *handle,
69	                              const box_t *box,
70	                              const char *file_path,
71	                              const char *fn_name,
72	                              const void **input_data,
73	                              const int   *input_sizes,
74	                              const int   *input_native,
75	                              int          n_inputs,
76	                              int          output_native,
77	                              void        *out_buf,
78	                              int          out_buf_capacity,
79	                              int         *out_size);
80	
81	/* `box` is the box record being invoked, or NULL when the caller has
82	 * no box context (e.g. test fixtures driving the spec directly).
83	 * Specs that need declared return type, compile hints, or future
84	 * sentinel-emission decisions read fields off `box`. Specs that
85	 * don't need it ignore the parameter. The pointer is valid only for
86	 * the duration of the call. */
87	
88	/* Wire-format bridge: convert one direction of native ↔ JSON.
89	 * Used when a value crosses a language boundary. Spec sets one
90	 * or both; if NULL, the dispatch layer treats native and JSON as
91	 * identical byte streams (the current uniform-byte default).
92	 *
93	 * `handle` is the worker's per-language handle (the same one
94	 * `invoke` receives) — bridges may need it to reach a runtime-side
95	 * scratch area: Lua reads/writes via the lua_State stack, C may
96	 * keep a typed-pointer table, Bash may need its sub-process. Specs
97	 * that don't need it ignore the argument.
98	 *
99	 * Stack-protocol convention for `native_to_json`: the producer
100	 * leaves its native value on top of its runtime's working store
101	 * (Lua: top of stack); the bridge consumes it. For `json_to_native`:
102	 * the bridge pushes the reconstructed value onto the same store.
103	 * `src` / `dst` carry only the JSON side. */
104	typedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(void *handle,
105	                              const void *src, int src_size,
106	                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
107	/* }}} */
108	
109	/* {{{ lang_spec_t */
110	typedef struct lang_spec {
111	    const char *name;       /* "lua", "bash", "c", "python", ... */
