210	## Runtime model — two paths, needs a decision
211	
212	Two implementation paths exist. The encapsulating box being a
213	normal `call` box from the editor's perspective doesn't pick
214	between them; the choice lives in what the map-language spec
215	does behind the scenes and what (if anything) the compile
216	pipeline does at build time.
217	
218	### Path A — Compile-time inlining
219	
220	The compile pipeline (issue 309) recognizes encapsulating call
221	boxes and **expands them into their sub-graphs at build time**.
222	Steps the compile step takes per encapsulating box:
223	
224	1. Load the sub-map's graph from `ref`.
225	2. Rename every internal box with a parent-namespaced id:
226	   `stats_pipeline__compute`, `stats_pipeline__filter`, etc.
227	3. Copy every internal wire across, with the renamed ids.
228	4. For each marked data box inside the sub-map: replace it with
229	   a wire that brings the encapsulating box's matching input
230	   value directly to wherever the data box's output was wired.
231	5. For each write box inside the sub-map: route its emitted
232	   value into the packed-blob writer that produces the
233	   encapsulating box's output (or, alternatively, expose each
234	   write box's value as a separate output wire that downstream
235	   consumers can wire to individually — see "Output unpacking
236	   under inlining" below).
237	6. Remove the encapsulating call box from the parent's graph.
238	
239	After expansion, the compiled artifact is **one flat graph**.
240	The runtime never sees `lang: "map"`. The map-language spec
241	exists only for the editor's sake (validating refs, listing
242	the sub-map's inputs/outputs for the inspector).
243	
244	**Pros:**
245	
246	- No new runtime machinery. The dispatch, slot store, and spec
247	  registry all operate on flat graphs as today.
248	- Best per-call performance — no sub-graph startup cost, no
249	  nested dispatch.
250	- **Composes cleanly with issue 313** (whole-program same-
251	  language merge). The inlined sub-graph's same-language regions
252	  merge into the parent's merge as if they were always one map.
253	- Debugging stack traces inside the inlined region point to
254	  flat box ids — readable once you know the naming convention.
255	
256	**Cons:**
257	
258	- The sub-map gets baked into every parent that uses it. Edit
259	  the sub-map → every parent must recompile.
260	- No hot-reload of sub-maps. The editor would have to
261	  re-expand on every parent map edit, or accept that the
262	  parent's last-compiled artifact lags the sub-map source.
263	- Compile time grows with sub-map size × number of
264	  encapsulations.
265	- Deep recursion (sub-maps inside sub-maps inside sub-maps)
266	  inflates the parent's compiled output proportionally.
267	- Output unpacking under inlining is awkward: the packed-blob
268	  output format makes sense at a boundary; with no boundary,
269	  downstream consumers either wire to individual sub-write-box
270	  outputs (more efficient but a different mental model) or get
271	  the packer's blob baked in too (the packer is just another
272	  inlined function call).
273	
274	### Path B — Runtime sub-graph dispatch
275	
276	The encapsulating box stays as one box in the parent's flat
277	graph. The map-language spec's `invoke` runs the sub-graph at
278	call time:
279	
280	1. **`map_init(worker_idx)`** — load every sub-map referenced
281	   by the running program (or lazily on first invoke). Each
282	   worker gets its own copy of the sub-graph state (slot store,
283	   dispatch context).
284	2. **`map_invoke(handle, sub_map_dir, ..., inputs, ..., out_buf, ...)`** —
285	   for each input, write the bytes into the shared-memory chunk
286	   that the matching marked data box reads from. Then drive the
287	   sub-graph to quiescence using the existing pool's dispatch
288	   loop (the sub-graph's tasks are normal pool tasks distinguished
289	   by a nested namespace). When quiescent, walk the write boxes,
290	   read their emitted values, pack them into the size-index +
291	   data-section blob, write the blob to `out_buf`.
292	
293	The sub-graph's parallelism uses the same thread pool as the
294	parent's. A sub-graph's tasks compete for workers alongside the
295	parent's tasks. From the pool's perspective, the difference is
296	just box-id prefix.
297	
298	**Pros:**
299	
300	- Sub-map loaded once per worker, reused across many parent
301	  invocations and across many parent maps.
302	- Hot-reload friendly: change the sub-map, parent picks up the
303	  new version on next run (no parent recompile).
304	- The packed-blob output format is the natural shape — the
305	  boundary exists, the spec packs at the boundary, downstream
306	  consumers unpack with the helper library.
307	- Debugging stack traces preserve the sub-map identity (the
308	  task's namespace says "this came from `stats-pipeline`").
309	- Recursion is natural — the spec's invoke can nest-invoke its
310	  own spec for a sub-sub-map.
311	
312	**Cons:**
313	
314	- New runtime machinery: the map spec's `invoke` runs a nested
315	  dispatch loop. Either the dispatch layer grows a re-entrancy
316	  contract, or the map spec replicates enough of the dispatch
317	  machinery to drive the sub-graph itself.
318	- Each encapsulating-box call pays sub-graph startup cost —
319	  seeding inputs, possibly resetting slot state from a previous
320	  invocation, collecting outputs at the end. Small but real.
321	- Does NOT compose with issue 313's same-language merge — the
322	  sub-map's source can't merge with the parent's, because at
323	  build time they're separate compilation units. (313 could be
324	  extended to merge across encapsulation boundaries, but that's
325	  a separate design.)
326	- Re-using the sub-graph across invocations requires reset
327	  semantics. Either the sub-graph's slots and counters get
328	  reset between invocations (cost), or each invocation gets a
329	  fresh sub-graph instance (allocation cost).
330	
331	### What to weigh
332	
333	The choice turns on whether encapsulation is **a build-time
334	abstraction (like a C macro or a Lua-function-inlined-by-LuaJIT)**
335	or **a runtime composition (like calling a function in a shared
336	library)**.
337	
338	Path A treats it as a build-time abstraction. Encapsulation is
339	how the user organizes source; the compiled artifact is one
340	flat program. Best for raw performance and integration with
341	the existing machinery. Cost: rebuild propagation.
342	
343	Path B treats it as a runtime composition. Encapsulation is a
344	real boundary at execution time, with the packed-blob output
345	as the wire format across the boundary. Best for modularity,
346	hot-reload, and clear semantics. Cost: per-call overhead and
347	loss of cross-boundary optimization.
348	
349	A third option exists — **support both, picked per encapsulating
350	box** via a small flag in the box JSON (`encap_mode: "inline" |
351	"runtime"`). The compile pipeline expands inline-mode boxes;
352	runtime-mode boxes stay encapsulated. Each user picks per
353	encapsulation based on their needs. Higher implementation cost
354	but maximally flexible. Probably not in the first slice; flag it
355	for later if either A or B alone proves too narrow.
356	
357	Recursion (a sub-map encapsulates another sub-map) works under
358	either path. Cycles between maps are a compile-time (or
359	load-time, under B) error.
360	
361	## Relationship to existing issues
362	
363	- **Issue 207 (source-file-browser)** — the file browser is the
364	  entry point for the "encapsulate" action.
```

### [1565] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T21:06:26.509Z

248's relationship to 313 is direct and is actually called out in 248's body. Here's the short version:

**248 in one line**: a sub-map becomes a single box on a parent map's canvas, exposing marked-as-external `read` boxes as inputs and `write` boxes as packed outputs. Sub-graphs become reusable, composable units.

**Two implementation paths in 248, and they differ exactly on 313**

**Path A — Compile-time inlining.** The compile pipeline expands the encapsulating box into its sub-graph at build time: rename internal boxes with a parent namespace, copy every internal wire, replace marked external-reads with direct wires from the parent's matching inputs, replace write boxes with the packed-output writer. Encapsulating box vanishes. Result: one flat graph at runtime. The runtime never sees `lang: "map"`.

This **composes cleanly with 313** — and the body says so directly: *"The inlined sub-graph's same-language regions merge into the parent's merge as if they were always one map."* Because once the sub-graph is inlined into the parent, its Lua boxes and the parent's Lua boxes look indistinguishable to 313's merger; they go into the same `__merged__.lua`. The encapsulation boundary disappears at compile time, so the language-merge boundary also disappears.

**Path B — Runtime sub-graph dispatch.** The encapsulating box stays one box. The map-language spec's `invoke` runs the sub-graph at call time, via a nested dispatch loop. Sub-map loaded once per worker; sub-graph's tasks compete for workers alongside the parent's tasks; outputs collected and packed at quiescence.

This **does NOT compose with 313** — body again: *"The sub-map's source can't merge with the parent's, because at build time they're separate compilation units."* The sub-map keeps its own compiled artifact (its own `__merged__.lua`); 313's merge stops at the encapsulation boundary.

**The relationship summarised**

- Path A treats encapsulation as a build-time abstraction. 313's same-language merger then operates across the (now flat) parent + inlined sub-graphs, getting more value because there's more same-language code to merge.
- Path B treats encapsulation as a runtime composition. 313's merger stops at every encapsulation boundary; each sub-map has its own merge.

The body even floats a Path C: a per-box `encap_mode: "inline" | "runtime"` flag so each encapsulation picks. That gives 313 the benefit on inline-mode boxes and isolates runtime-mode ones.

**One other thing worth flagging while we're here**

248 still uses `kind: "data"` in its body (the old terminology). Issue 229 renamed `data` → `read`/`write` long ago, and 244 made read boxes pull-on-demand. The body's "marked data boxes" should be "marked read boxes," and the description of how external inputs flow into them — *"a small shared-memory chunk seeded by the encapsulating call's invoke; the marked data box reads from that chunk on each fire"* — is essentially 244's read-box `cached_value`, just seeded by the parent at invoke time instead of by graph load. The terminology drift would be worth fixing if/when 248 moves forward.

**Where you are with 248**

It's open. No implementation. The path decision (A vs B vs both) is the load-bearing question; everything else (UI, file layout, output packing) waits on that. Scoping it would mean either picking a path or building Path C's flag-based "support both."

What angle are you on it from — picking the path, scoping the work, or just mapping how the issues connect?

### [1573] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T21:12:21.541Z

Yes, this works cleanly — and your framing of the two use cases is exactly the lens for picking per-encapsulation. Let me sketch what "both" actually looks like.

**The flag and the two use cases**

The encapsulating box's JSON carries an `encap_mode` field:

```json
{
  "id": "stats",
  "kind": "call",
  "lang": "map",
  "ref": "maps/stats-pipeline",
  "encap_mode": "inline",          // or "runtime"
  "inputs":  [...],
  "outputs": [...]
}
```

- `"inline"` → "I know this sub-map at compile time, I want it folded into the parent, and I want 313 to merge across the boundary."
- `"runtime"` → "I'm picking this sub-map by name at run time, or I want it hot-reloadable, or I want it isolated."

A user's typical run-time case maps to this: a `read` box feeds a name string into the encapsulating box; the spec resolves "load `maps/<that-name>`" lazily. The parent can't know the name at compile time, so `inline` isn't an option — `runtime` is forced.

A user's typical compile-time case: the user dragged a known sub-map from the file browser onto the canvas. The reference is static; `inline` is the natural default; the compiled artifact stays one piece.

**What the compile pipeline does for each**

`scripts/soramech-compile.sh` grows a graph-rewrite pass *before* the existing source-merge and per-spec packaging. For every encapsulating box in the parent:

- If `encap_mode == "inline"`:
  1. Load the sub-map's graph.
  2. Namespace every internal box id (`stats__filter`, `stats__compute`).
  3. Copy every wire across with the renamed ids.
  4. For each marked external-input `read` box in the sub-map, replace it with a direct wire from the parent's matching input on the encapsulating box.
  5. For each `write` box in the sub-map, either pack into a blob (preserving the boundary's output shape) or expose individually as separate outputs from the encapsulating-box site.
  6. Delete the encapsulating box from the parent's box list.
  7. Recurse: if the sub-map itself contains inline encapsulations, expand those too.

After this pass, the parent's flat graph is what the rest of the pipeline (and 313's merge step) sees. 313's `merge_lua_sources` then groups same-language boxes — including the ones that came from the inlined sub-map — into one module.

- If `encap_mode == "runtime"`:
  1. Leave the encapsulating box alone.
  2. Also compile the sub-map's own artifacts (its own `__merged__.lua`, its own C `.so`s) into a side directory the parent's runtime can find.
  3. The encapsulating box stays a `lang: "map"` call box in the parent's flat graph.

A single parent can have both. The pipeline iterates every box, decides per-box, runs the right transform.

**What the runtime does for each**

For `inline` boxes: nothing special. They're already flat in the compiled artifact; the dispatch sees ordinary boxes.

For `runtime` boxes: the map-language spec — `langs/map/spec.so` — implements the standard `lang_spec_t`:

- `map_init(worker_idx)`: empty cache of loaded sub-graphs.
- `map_invoke(handle, box, sub_map_path, fn_name, inputs, ..., out_buf, ...)`:
  1. Resolve the sub-map (cache key = path or runtime-supplied name). On miss, `graph_load(path)`, `graph_attach_runtime(...)` against a *fresh* per-worker slot store dedicated to this sub-graph instance.
  2. For each input, seed the matching marked external-`read` box's `cached_value` with the bytes the parent supplied.
  3. Drive the sub-graph to quiescence on the *same pool* — its tasks share workers with the parent. Per-task box ids get a nested-namespace prefix so traces stay distinguishable.
  4. Walk the `write` boxes, collect their captured outputs, pack into the size-indexed blob format, write to `out_buf`.
  5. Optionally reset slot state for re-invocation (cheaper than re-loading the whole sub-graph).

This is exactly Path B from 248's body, modular and self-contained.

**Where 313 sits in the picture**

- Inline-mode boxes contribute their source files to the parent's `__merged__.lua`. 313's existing merger handles them uniformly — it doesn't even need to know about encapsulation; it just sees more boxes after the inline pass ran.
- Runtime-mode boxes get their own `__merged__.lua` in their own compiled directory. 313 merges within each sub-map's region but doesn't cross the boundary.

**The schema constraint, which "both" doesn't escape**

Even in runtime mode, the parent needs to wire the encapsulating box's input ports and output channels at compile time. So the encapsulating box's JSON must declare the schema: input port names, output channel names, output blob layout. Sub-maps loaded at runtime have to *match* that schema (the spec checks at load time; mismatch is a fatal error).

This is like a function-pointer typedef in C — you can swap which function the pointer holds at run time, but the signature is fixed in source. For maps, the analogue is: the encapsulating box says "this sub-map will have inputs A/B/C and outputs X/Y," and any sub-map referenced by name at runtime has to satisfy that contract.

**Cost summary, honest**

- The map-language spec is built either way (it's the foundation for runtime mode and the dynamic-load case is your stated motivating use).
- Inline-mode is a graph-rewrite step in the compile pipeline. Probably 100–200 lines of clean code: load the sub-map, namespace, splice, recurse. Lives in `soramech-compile.sh` or a sibling.
- The encap_mode flag is one field in the box JSON, one branch in the rewrite pass, one branch in the dispatch's "is this a real call or a runtime sub-graph?" decision (and the latter is just "what spec answers this `lang`").
- Output packing/unpacking is the same helper library either way (used at the boundary in runtime mode, used at the inlined `write`-replacement in inline mode if the user wants the blob format).

So "both" is roughly **Path B's work plus a graph-rewrite pass for Path A's transform**. It's bigger than either path alone, but the *runtime* surface area is just Path B's spec; everything Path A adds happens at compile time and disappears before the runtime sees the graph. That separation keeps "both" from compounding complexity at the hot path — the dispatch loop never branches on `encap_mode`.

**One last thing worth surfacing**

If `encap_mode` is a per-box choice, the user can mix freely in one parent. Stats-pipeline as inline (perf-critical, known at compile time); user-template-loader as runtime (dynamic, hot-reloadable). The semantics are the same from the parent's perspective — both look like a call box with declared inputs and outputs. The difference is invisible to the parent's other boxes; it only shows up in build output (one is gone, one stays) and in the runtime profile (one calls inline, one calls through the map spec).

That clean separation — "both modes look the same to the rest of the parent" — is what makes "both" cheaper than it sounds. The encapsulating box's *contract* is the same; only the implementation behind it varies.

What's your read — is the schema-at-compile-time constraint OK for the runtime use case you have in mind?

_tool_result:_
```
1	# 246 — Custom translation specs per input port
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Concept
7	
8	The default language specs (issues 306 / 307 / 308) handle most
9	data flow via the dual-ring shape in issue 312: producer's spec
10	serializes native or JSON per the per-edge bit; consumer's spec
11	dispatches per input flag and decodes accordingly. The default
12	contract is sufficient for primitives, strings, arrays, and
13	objects — the cases JSON's structure covers cleanly and the cases
14	native pass-through covers cleanly.
15	
16	A custom translation spec lets the user intervene at a specific
17	input port for **any reason** — not just cross-language
18	ambiguity. Two motivating cases:
19	
20	**Cross-language ambiguity.** Some translations cannot be
21	inferred by the spec because the language constructs don't have
22	universal mappings:
23	
24	- A C++ class with both data fields and method functions arrives
25	  at a C box. C has no notion of methods; whether to expose them
26	  as function pointers, drop them, or restructure into a tagged
27	  union is a development-time decision the user has to make.
28	- A `void *` arrives carrying a libcurl handle the receiving
29	  language's spec has no general way to interpret.
30	- A union type whose discriminator lives in a separate input
31	  port — the spec doesn't know which port is the tag.
32	
33	**Same-language transformation the user wants.** Even when no
34	language boundary is crossed, the default native pass-through
35	may not be what the user wants on a particular port:
36	
37	- A C box emits a float; a downstream C box wants it truncated
38	  and rounded up before consumption. The default native pass
39	  copies the bytes verbatim and downstream code sees the wrong
40	  precision. A custom shim on the destination port applies
41	  truncate-then-round before invoke sees the value. No language
42	  ambiguity — just user-specific semantics at that wire.
43	- A consumer wants units normalized on one port but not on a
44	  sibling port (degrees → radians on the angle input, leave the
45	  magnitude input alone).
46	- A consumer wants to clamp or range-check a value before its
47	  function sees it.
48	
49	In both cases the principle is the same: **the user has knowledge
50	about how this specific port should interpret its input, and the
51	default spec doesn't.** A per-port shim is where that knowledge
52	gets written down.
53	
54	**A custom translation spec is a per-input-port shim** the user
55	writes to handle these cases. It runs at the start of the
56	consumer box's task, replaces the default decode (or pass-through)
57	for that one port, and otherwise leaves the dispatch path
58	unchanged.
59	
60	### Why per-port, not per-box
61	
62	> "the custom spec should be defined on a per-slot basis. Each
63	> slot should (ideally) only need to handle one particular type
64	> of information. That's why we have separate arguments at all,
65	> instead of just using void pointers for everything." —
66	> 2026-05-21
67	
68	Per-port matches the principled reason input ports exist in the
69	first place: each port carries one kind of value, and the user
70	declares the kind. If a box has three input ports — two of them
71	ordinary strings and one of them a C++ class instance — only the
72	class-instance port needs custom translation. The two string ports
73	keep the default decode. A per-box shim would force the user to
74	write decode code for every port even when only one is weird.
75	
76	### Why no output-side custom translation
77	
78	A producer's spec always knows how to serialize every one of its
79	own language's values losslessly — Lua's spec knows every Lua type,
80	C's spec knows every typed struct field. Writing depends only on
81	the sending language, which has full reflective access to its own
82	type system. The Lua spec's `encode_value` is expected to grow
83	sentinel primitives (`$function_pointer`, `$lang_opaque`) for the
84	values JSON can't represent structurally; that's a spec-author
85	responsibility, not a user-shim responsibility.
86	
87	Reading is the asymmetric direction. The receiver doesn't know
88	what the sender meant by an opaque pointer or a sentinel-tagged
89	value — that interpretation is the user's call. Hence the custom
90	shim lives on the input side only.
91	
92	## Lifecycle (editor)
93	
94	The box inspector's input-port rows each grow a small affordance
95	for managing the port's custom translation:
96	
97	- **New** — creates a translation file in
98	  `maps/<name>/translations/<box_id>__<port_name>.<lang_ext>`
99	  and sets the port's `custom_translation` field in the box JSON
100	  to that path. The file is initialized to a copy of what the
101	  default spec would do for that port's declared type — the user
102	  can run it without edits and behaviour is identical to no
103	  custom shim. Edits add or replace the decode logic.
104	- **View** — opens the translation file in the editor's read-only
105	  source view (same affordance as "view source" on the box's main
106	  function). The user-facing message is "this is what gets baked
107	  into the map at compile time."
108	- **Delete** — clears the port's `custom_translation` field. The
109	  translation file itself stays on disk. Box-delete (the whole
110	  box) with any custom translations attached prompts: "this box
111	  has N custom translation specs; the files stay on disk for
112	  re-use, but the box's references will go." Confirm or cancel.
113	
114	The "file stays on disk if you delete the box" rule means custom
115	translations are identifiable by path, not by box. The user can
116	attach the same file to a different box's port later, or delete
117	the file explicitly through a file-browser pane.
118	
119	## Lifecycle (runtime)
120	
121	The dispatch's input-read step gains a per-port branch:
122	
123	```
124	for each input port i:
125	    (which_ring, idx) = ring_order_pop(slot)
126	    raw_bytes         = ring_at(...)
127	    if box->inputs[i].custom_translation:
128	        translated[i] = custom_translation[i](raw_bytes, which_ring)
129	        input_native[i] = NATIVE   # custom shim output is always native
130	    else:
131	        translated[i] = raw_bytes
132	        input_native[i] = (which_ring == NATIVE)
133	```
134	
135	When the port has a custom shim, the shim does the decode and the
136	spec's `invoke` sees native bytes (with `input_native[i] = NATIVE`).
137	When the port has no custom shim, the default decode runs inside
138	the spec's invoke as today.
139	
140	Three consequences fall out from running on the consumer's worker:
141	
142	- A custom shim that does something slow or surprising holds the
143	  consumer's worker, not the producer's. The producer is free as
144	  soon as it's done writing raw bytes to the ring.
145	- Each consumer eats its own translation cost; mixed fan-in
146	  consumers translate cell-by-cell as they pop.
147	- The custom shim is in the same language as the box's main
148	  function, so it has full access to that language's runtime
149	  (Lua tables, C structs, etc.) without crossing another
150	  language boundary first.
151	
152	## Where it sits relative to the dual-ring (issue 312)
153	
154	The dual-ring routing still applies. The producer chooses a ring
155	per `output_edge_native[j]` as today. The consumer's per-port
156	custom shim sees the raw bytes plus the `which_ring` tag — so it
157	can distinguish "these bytes are my own language's native format"
158	from "these bytes are JSON from a cross-language producer" and act
159	accordingly.
160	
161	In the easy cases (same-language same-shape), no custom shim is
162	needed; the default native pass-through still works. The custom
163	shim earns its keep on the cells where the default
164	`json_to_native` would error or produce something useless — that's
165	where the user-written code takes over.
166	
167	## File layout
168	
169	```
170	maps/<name>/
```

```
200	
201	## Custom translation function signature
202	
203	Per-port shims take one input's raw bytes and the `which_ring`
204	flag, return one translated value in the consumer's native form.
205	Working draft for C:
206	
207	```c
208	int sm_translate(const void *input_raw, int input_size,
209	                 int input_native,
210	                 void *output_native, int output_capacity,
211	                 int *output_size);
212	```
213	
214	Lua's shape is analogous but in Lua-native terms — the function
215	takes a Lua string (the raw bytes) and a boolean (the native
216	flag), returns the translated Lua value.
217	
218	One signature per port keeps each shim's surface area exactly
219	matched to the port's job. A shim that needs to consult other
220	ports has a problem this design doesn't try to solve (per-port
221	isolation is the principled choice); the user can fall back to
222	plain decode-then-validate inside the box's main function for
223	cross-port logic.
224	
225	## Default content of a freshly-created shim
226	
227	The "New" button writes a file containing a literal copy of what
228	the default spec would do for that port's declared type. For C,
229	that's the small generated wrapper that parses `input_raw` per
230	the port's declared type and writes the result. For Lua, it's a
231	few lines that decode via the existing JSON / native helpers
232	based on `input_native`.
233	
234	The user can run the shim without edits and behaviour matches the
235	no-shim default. Edits typically replace one branch (the
236	specific weird case) and leave the rest alone. The "starts as
237	default, edits in place" shape matches the project's preference
238	for upgrade paths over from-scratch authoring.
239	
240	## Compile pipeline (issue 309)
241	
242	The compile pipeline already walks every box's `ref` to copy
243	source into `compiled/src/`. The same walk inspects each input
244	port's `custom_translation` field and copies referenced files
245	into `compiled/translations/`. For C, each translation file
246	compiles to a `.so` alongside the box's main function. For Lua,
247	files load into the worker's `lua_State` like any other Lua
248	source.
249	
250	When issue 313 (same-language source merge) lands, translation
251	files get concatenated into the per-language bundle the same way
252	box main functions do — a translation file is just another
253	function in the merged module.
254	
255	## Why this is the right escape hatch
256	
257	The default spec contract is "JSON is the universal interop, each
258	spec handles its own native form, sentinels carry the in-process
259	exotic cases on the producer side." That's enough for the
260	overwhelming majority of data flow. The cases where it fails are
261	exactly the cases where the user has knowledge the system can't
262	ever infer — what a `void *` points to, how a serialized object
263	is structured, which port is a discriminator for which other port.
264	
265	Pushing those back to the user via a per-port shim is honest
266	about where the knowledge has to come from. It also lets the
267	default spec contract stay narrow and predictable — the spec
268	authors don't have to chase every possible ambiguous receive-side
269	type with new special cases.
270	
271	## Relevant files
272	
273	- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — box state; per-input
274	  `custom_translation` field read/write.
275	- `assets/index.html` — inspector UI (New / View / Delete buttons
276	  on each input-port row).
277	- `src/006-server-main.lua` — HTTP backend for translation file
278	  CRUD (path validation against `maps/<name>/translations/`).
279	- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — load each input port's
280	  `custom_translation` onto `box_t->inputs[i]`.
281	- `src/012-dispatch.c` — input-read branch on
282	  `box->inputs[i].custom_translation`.
283	- `scripts/soramech-compile.sh` — package translation files into
284	  the compiled map (issue 309).
285	- `issues/245-wire-color-by-cross-language-classification.md` —
286	  goldenrod-cheddar wires for ports with custom translations.
287	- `issues/312-same-language-wire-fast-path.md` — dual-ring shape
288	  the translation slots into.
289	- `issues/313-research-whole-program-same-language-merge.md` —
290	  custom translations get merged alongside box source.
291	
292	## Suggested implementation sequence
293	
294	1. **Box JSON schema**: add the optional per-input
295	   `custom_translation` field. Graph loader reads it; absence
296	   means default path for that port.
297	2. **HTTP backend**: CRUD routes for translation files. Path
298	   validation against `maps/<name>/translations/`.
299	3. **Editor inspector**: New / View / Delete affordances on each
300	   input-port row. "New" generates the file with
301	   default-spec-equivalent contents parameterized on the port's
302	   declared type. The 245 wire-coloring change activates
303	   automatically once the field exists.
304	4. **Box-delete confirmation**: detect any ports with attached
305	   translations; prompt before deletion; preserve the files on
306	   disk regardless.
307	5. **Dispatch**: input-read per-port branch on
308	   `custom_translation`; route bytes through the shim instead of
309	   the default decode for that port.
310	6. **Compile pipeline**: walk each input port's
311	   `custom_translation`; package files into
312	   `compiled/translations/` and the per-language source bundle.
313	
314	## Open questions
315	
316	- **Shared translations across multiple ports.** Two ports on
317	  different boxes that need the same shim can both reference the
318	  same file path — the system supports it implicitly. The
319	  editor's "New" button always creates a fresh file with the
320	  default naming so users don't accidentally share by default.
321	  Library imports inside translation files (for shared helpers
322	  inside a shim) are out of scope here — the user can do that via
323	  the language's normal import mechanism.
324	- **Compile-time validation.** Should the compile button check
325	  that every `custom_translation` path exists and parses? Easy
326	  add to the existing compile validation; do once translations
327	  exist as a feature.
328	- **Shim author seeing cross-port context.** Per-port isolation
329	  means a shim only sees its own input. A union whose

### [1592] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-22T21:21:08.990Z

**246 in short**

A per-input-port shim the user writes — runs before the consumer's `invoke` sees the value, intercepts raw bytes plus the `which_ring` tag, and produces translated native bytes the spec then receives as if the default decode had produced them.

Two distinct motivating cases — and you can see why they share the same mechanism:

- **Cross-language ambiguity**: a C++ class arriving at a C box, a `void *` carrying a libcurl handle, a tagged union whose discriminator is on a separate port. JSON's structure can't carry these losslessly; the user has knowledge the spec can't ever infer. Per-port shim is where that knowledge lives.
- **Same-language user transformation**: a C box emits a float; the next C box wants it truncated-then-rounded. Or degrees → radians on one port. Or clamp-to-range. No language boundary, just user semantics that don't belong in the box's main function.

**The principled choices behind the design**

- **Per-port, not per-box.** Each port carries one kind of value; only the weird ports get shims. A box with three string inputs and one C++ class input writes one shim, not four.
- **Input-side only.** A producer's spec always knows how to serialise its own language's values — full reflective access to its own type system. Reading is the asymmetric direction: the receiver can't infer what the sender meant by an opaque pointer. So shims live where the ambiguity is.
- **Shim's output is always treated as native by `invoke`.** The shim does the decode; the consumer's main function sees native bytes, same as if a same-language producer had pushed them.

**Where it sits architecturally**

Slots into the existing dual-ring shape from 312. The shim sees both the raw bytes AND the per-cell `which_ring` tag, so it can branch on "this is JSON from cross-lang" vs "this is native from same-lang" inside the shim. The default decode runs when no shim is attached — nothing about the existing fast path changes.

Composes with 313's merge: translation files are just another function in the same-language merged module.

Composes with 245's wire-colouring (the goldenrod-cheddar colour activates automatically once a port has a custom translation attached — visual marker that this wire is non-default).

**Status, honestly**

Open. No implementation. Nothing blocks it:

- 312's dual-ring is in place (shim's `which_ring` argument has its source).
- The dispatch's per-port read loop exists (the branch hook lives in `read_inputs`).
- The compile pipeline (309) already walks per-box source; extending the walk to per-port translation files is the same pattern.
- The editor side has the inspector infrastructure for input-port rows.

**Implementation shape, ~6 slices in order of dependency**

The body lays out a sensible sequence:
1. Box JSON schema gains optional per-input `custom_translation` path field. Loader reads it onto `box_t->inputs[i]`. Pure data plumbing.
2. HTTP backend CRUD for translation files (editor-side).
3. Editor inspector New / View / Delete buttons.
4. Box-delete confirmation when ports have attached translations.
5. Dispatch input-read branch: if `custom_translation` set, route through the shim before default decode.
6. Compile pipeline packages translation files into `compiled/translations/` and per-language source bundle.

Slices 1, 5, 6 are the C runtime work — that's the path that makes the feature *usable* from hand-edited box JSON without any editor UI. Slices 2, 3, 4 are the editor side that makes it ergonomic. The minimum-viable feature could ship as just 1 + 5 + 6.

**One thing the body acknowledges as a hard limit**

A shim only sees its own port's bytes. Cross-port logic (a union with a discriminator on a sibling port) doesn't fit the per-port isolation. The body's stance: that case falls back to plain decode-then-validate inside the box's main function. Honest — per-port is the principled abstraction, and cross-port logic is what the main function already exists for.

**Where you might be in deciding about it**

- Schema-and-runtime slice (1 + 5 + 6) is small and clean — maybe a few hundred lines of C across loader, dispatch, and the compile script. Solid foundation.
- Editor slice (2 + 3 + 4) is JavaScript work; separately scoped.
- The whole thing is opt-in per port; existing maps don't change.

What's your angle on it — scoping the runtime slice, or just mapping the design?
